Field Theory of Active Brownian Particles in Potentials
Ziluo Zhang, Lili Fehértói-Nagy, Maria Polackova, Gunnar Pruessner

Abstract
The Active Brownian Particle (ABP) model exemplifies a wide class of active matter particles. In this work, we demonstrate how this model can be cast into a field theory in both two and three dimensions. Our aim is manifold: we wish both to extract useful features of the system, as well as to build a framework which can be used to study more complex systems involving ABPs, such as those involving interaction. Using the two-dimensional model as a template, we calculate the mean squared displacement exactly, and the one-point density in an external potential perturbatively. We show how the effective diffusion constant appears in the barometric density formula to leading order, and determine the corrections to it. We repeat the calculation in three dimensions, clearly a more challenging setup. Comparing different ways to capture the self-propulsion, we find that its perturbative treatment results in more tractable derivations without loss of exactness, where this is accessible.



I
Introduction

Active Brownian particles (ABPs)
Howse:2007

are one of the paradigmatic models of active matter
Bechinger:2016
.
Such particles move with a constant self-propulsion
speed

w_{0}
, in a time-varying direction, represented by a vector subject to rotational diffusion with constant
\mathsf{D}_{r}
. At the same time, the particles are subject to thermal diffusion with constant
\mathsf{D}_{t}

Cates:2013
;
Cates:2015
.
As opposed to Run-and-Tumble (RnT) motion
Cates:2013
, both degrees of freedom, the vectorial velocity
\mathbf{w}(t)
as well as the position
\mathbf{r}(t)
, are continuous functions of time
t
.
ABPs approximate so-called Janus particles particularly well
Howse:2007
;
Bechinger:2016
. Similarly,
monotrichous
bacteria vary their orientation more smoothly and are much more reliant on rotational diffusion
LiTamTang:2008
. In contrast, the motion of the much-studied
E. coli
is closer to RnT.

The motion of ABPs is most directly described by a pair of Langevin equations for their position
\mathbf{r}(t)
as a function of time
t
,

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\mathbf{r}(t)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{w}(t)+\bm{\xi}(t)

with

\displaystyle\left\langle\bm{\xi}(t)\cdot\bm{\xi}(t^{\prime})\right\rangle

\displaystyle=2d\mathsf{D}_{t}\delta(t^{\prime}-t)

(1a)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\mathbf{w}(t)

\displaystyle=\bm{\zeta}_{\perp\mathbf{w}(t)}(t)

with

\displaystyle\left\langle\bm{\zeta}_{\perp\mathbf{w}(t)}(t)\cdot\bm{\zeta}_{\perp\mathbf{w}(t^{\prime})}(t^{\prime})\right\rangle

\displaystyle=2(d-1)w_{0}^{2}\mathsf{D}_{r}\delta(t^{\prime}-t)

(1b)

where the angular brackets
\left\langle\bullet\right\rangle
indicate an ensemble average. Both
\bm{\xi}(t)
and
\bm{\zeta}_{\perp\mathbf{w}(t)}(t)
represent white noise with vanishing mean and correlators as stated.
The translational noise
\bm{\xi}(t)
is the commonly used Gaussian displacement in
d
dimensions.
The rotational noise
\bm{\zeta}_{\perp\mathbf{w}(t)}(t)
is confined to the
(d-1)
-dimensional tangential plane orthogonal to
\mathbf{w}(t)
at any time

Carlsson:2010
, so that
\mathbf{w}(t)
stays on the surface of a sphere of radius
w_{0}
at any time. This is guaranteed by
\left\langle\mathbf{w}(t)\cdot\bm{\zeta}_{\perp\mathbf{w}(t)}(t)\right\rangle=0
,
which requires an Ito interpretation of Eq. (
1b
). In Section
I.1
we cast the above in Fokker-Planck form, avoiding any such issues of stochastic calculus.

The motion of ABPs in two dimensions has been analysed in various ways using classical methods
FrankeGruler:1990
;
Cates:2013
;
Cates:2015
;
Bechinger:2016
, while ABPs in three dimensions have received much less attention. This might be related to the complications arising from the curvature of the surface the rotational diffusion takes place on. The aim of the present work is to develop the mathematical toolkit that will allow us to study ABPs using field theory. Some of us have studied ABPs in two dimensions before
ZhangPruessner:2022
. We will now study ABPs in two dimensions from a different perspective, primarily to provide us with a template for the treatment in three dimensions.

Studying ABPs in two and three dimensions through field theory will not only give direct insight about ABPs — developing and verifying this framework will also open the door to add interaction. We take a first step in this work by considering external potentials.

There are, in principle, always two ways of describing single-particle dynamics using field theory. Either, one can use a suitable eigensystem in which to expand the fields, or proceed by considering the motion as a perturbation about pure diffusion. The former approach is, in theory, more powerful, as the particle dynamics is fully contained in a set of eigenfunctions, whose eigenvalues determine the “decay” rates. However, a combination of the two approaches is sometimes called for, particularly when there are multiple intertwined degrees of freedom, not all of which can be included in an appropriate eigensystem. The motion of Run-and-Tumble particles in a harmonic potential is an example of such a system
Garcia-MillanPruessner:2021
. In the present case of ABPs, the choice seems obvious in two dimensions, as, after suitable simplifications, we encounter the Mathieu equation. However, in three dimensions, no such simplifications are available. This necessitates exploring new methods, in particular determining the eigenfunctions themselves perturbatively, or treating self-propulsion as a perturbation about pure diffusion. This latter method is first tried out in the easier case of two dimensions.

The complexity of the methods increases further when we allow for an external potential, which must be treated perturbatively. As a test bed for the methods, we return to ABPs in two dimensions
ZhangPruessner:2022
, before moving on to three dimensions. In both cases, we determine the mean squared displacement (MSD) in closed form, as well as the stationary density in the presence of an external potential. These two observables are related: the MSD naturally gives rise to the notion of an
effective diffusion constant
, which may or may not feature as the effective temperature in a Boltzmann-like distribution of particles in the stationary state.

In the following, we introduce the ABP model, first through a Fokker-Planck description, and subsequently in the form of an action. In Section
II
, we proceed to characterise the MSD and stationary distribution in two dimensions. We do this first using Mathieu functions, and then perturbatively. These methods and results serve as the template for the treatment of ABPs in three dimensions in Section
III
.

I.1
Active Brownian Particles

Active Brownian Particles (ABP) are particles that move by self-propulsion by a constant speed
w_{0}
as as well as due to thermal noise characterised by translational diffusion constant
\mathsf{D}_{t}
. The direction of the self-propulsion is itself subject to diffusion. In the following, we will generally denote this direction by
\Omega
, and more specifically in the plane the polar angle by
\varphi\in[0,2\pi)
and in spherical coordinates the azimuthal angle by
\varphi\in[0,2\pi)
and the polar angle by
\theta\in[0,\pi)
. The direction of the self-propulsion is then

\mathbf{w}(\Omega)=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}w_{x}\\
w_{y}\end{array}\right)=w_{0}\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\cos\varphi\\
\sin\varphi\end{array}\right)\quad\text{ and }\quad\mathbf{w}(\Omega)=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}w_{x}\\
w_{y}\\
w_{z}\end{array}\right)=w_{0}\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\sin\theta\cos\varphi\\
\sin\theta\sin\varphi\\
\cos\theta\end{array}\right)

(2)

in two and three dimensions respectively. The rotational diffusion is parameterised by the constant
\mathsf{D}_{r}
, specifically by a Fokker-Planck equation of the form
\partial_{t}P(\Omega,t)=\mathsf{D}_{r}\nabla^{2}_{\Omega}P(\Omega,t)
with

\nabla^{2}_{\Omega}=\partial_{\varphi}^{2}\quad\text{ and }\quad\nabla^{2}_{\Omega}=\frac{1}{\sin\theta}\partial_{\theta}\sin\!\theta\ \partial_{\theta}+\frac{1}{\sin^{2}\!\theta}\partial_{\varphi}^{2}

(3)

in two and three dimensions respectively.

The Fokker-Planck equation of active Brownian particles is

\partial_{t}P(\mathbf{r},\Omega,t)=\mathcal{L}P(\mathbf{r},\Omega,t)\quad\text{with}\quad\mathcal{L}=\mathsf{D}_{t}\nabla_{\mathbf{r}}^{2}-\mathbf{w}(\Omega)\cdot\nabla_{\mathbf{r}}+\nabla_{\mathbf{r}}\cdot\Big(\nabla_{\mathbf{r}}\Upsilon(\mathbf{r})\Big)+\mathsf{D}_{r}\nabla^{2}_{\Omega}

(4)

where
P(\mathbf{r},\Omega,t)
is the probability to find a particle after some initialization at time
t
at position
\mathbf{r}
with the director pointing to
\Omega
and
\nabla_{\mathbf{r}}
the usual spatial gradient as opposed to
\nabla_{\Omega}
acting on the director, Eq. (
3
). The self-propulsion is implemented by the term
\mathbf{w}\cdot\nabla_{\mathbf{r}}
, which is the only term mixing the degrees of freedom
\Omega
and
\mathbf{r}
. The effect of an additional external potential
\Upsilon(\mathbf{r})
which acts equally on particles without orientation is implemented by
\nabla_{\mathbf{r}}\cdot(\nabla_{\mathbf{r}}\Upsilon)
, which is structurally identical to that of a drift
\mathbf{w}
and reduces to that term when
\nabla_{\mathbf{r}}\Upsilon
is constant in space. We write
\Upsilon^{\prime}
for
\nabla_{\mathbf{r}}\Upsilon
in the following. When the operator
\nabla_{\mathbf{r}}\cdot\Upsilon^{\prime}
acts on
P
in Eq. (
4
), only the leftmost spatial gradient
\nabla_{\mathbf{r}}
acts on the product of
\Upsilon^{\prime}
and
P
, whereas
\nabla_{\mathbf{r}}\Upsilon
results solely in
\Upsilon^{\prime}
.
For ease of notation below, we further introduce

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{1}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{D}_{t}\nabla_{\mathbf{r}}^{2}-\mathbf{w}(\Omega)\cdot\nabla_{\mathbf{r}}+\mathsf{D}_{r}\nabla^{2}_{\Omega}

(5a)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{2}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{D}_{t}\nabla_{\mathbf{r}}^{2}+\mathsf{D}_{r}\nabla^{2}_{\Omega}\ .

(5b)

From the single-particle Fokker-Planck Eq. (
4
) the multiple, non-interacting particle action

\mathcal{A}[\tilde{\chi},\chi]=\int\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}^{d}r\,}{\!\mathrm{d}^{d}r\,}{\!\mathrm{d}^{d}r\,}{\!\mathrm{d}^{d}r\,}\int\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}\Omega\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\Omega\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\Omega\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\Omega\,}\int\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}t\,}{\!\mathrm{d}t\,}{\!\mathrm{d}t\,}{\!\mathrm{d}t\,}\tilde{\chi}(\mathbf{r},\Omega,t)\big(-\partial_{t}+\mathcal{L}-r\big)\chi(\mathbf{r},\Omega,t)

(6)

follows immediately
PruessnerGarcia-Millan:2022
, with an additional mass
r\downarrow 0
as a regulator. At this stage, we leave the dimension
d\in\{2,3\}
and the integral
\int\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}\Omega\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\Omega\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\Omega\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\Omega\,}
unspecified. Expectations of the annihilator field
\chi(\mathbf{r},\Omega,t)
and the creator field
\tilde{\chi}(\mathbf{r},\Omega,t)
can be calculated in a path integral
PruessnerGarcia-Millan:2022

\left\langle\bullet\right\rangle=\int\mathcal{D}\!\chi\,\mathcal{D}\!\tilde{\chi}\,\mathchoice{\mathrm{e}^{\mathcal{A}[\tilde{\chi},\chi]}}{\operatorname{exp}\left(\mathcal{A}[\tilde{\chi},\chi]\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(\mathcal{A}[\tilde{\chi},\chi]\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(\mathcal{A}[\tilde{\chi},\chi]\right)}\bullet\ =\left\langle\bullet\mathchoice{\mathrm{e}^{-\mathcal{A}_{P}[\tilde{\chi},\chi]}}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-\mathcal{A}_{P}[\tilde{\chi},\chi]\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-\mathcal{A}_{P}[\tilde{\chi},\chi]\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-\mathcal{A}_{P}[\tilde{\chi},\chi]\right)}\right\rangle_{0}\quad\text{with}\quad\left\langle\bullet\right\rangle_{0}=\int\mathcal{D}\!\tilde{\chi}\,\mathcal{D}\!\chi\,\bullet\mathchoice{\mathrm{e}^{-\mathcal{A}_{0}[\tilde{\chi},\chi]}}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-\mathcal{A}_{0}[\tilde{\chi},\chi]\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-\mathcal{A}_{0}[\tilde{\chi},\chi]\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-\mathcal{A}_{0}[\tilde{\chi},\chi]\right)}\ ,

(7)

which allows for a perturbative treatment by splitting the action into a harmonic part
\mathcal{A}_{0}
, whose path-integral is readily taken, and a perturbative part
\mathcal{A}_{P}
, whose exponential is expanded,

\left\langle\bullet\right\rangle=\int\mathcal{D}\!\chi\,\mathcal{D}\!\tilde{\chi}\,\mathchoice{\mathrm{e}^{\mathcal{A}_{0}[\tilde{\chi},\chi]}}{\operatorname{exp}\left(\mathcal{A}_{0}[\tilde{\chi},\chi]\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(\mathcal{A}_{0}[\tilde{\chi},\chi]\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(\mathcal{A}_{0}[\tilde{\chi},\chi]\right)}\bullet\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{\mathcal{A}_{P}^{n}}{n!}=\left\langle\bullet\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{\mathcal{A}_{P}^{n}}{n!}\right\rangle_{0}\ ,

(8)

where we have introduced
\left\langle\bullet\right\rangle_{0}
as an average on the basis of the harmonic part of the action only.
Which part of the action Eq. (
6
) is treated perturbatively depends crucially on the eigensystem the fields
\chi
and
\tilde{\chi}
are written in, as it determines which part of the operator becomes diagonal. Because the external potential is arbitrary, it cannot be diagonalised in its present generality.

We may thus consider two schemes: In the first, the fields are written in eigenfunctions of
\mathcal{L}_{1}
, Eq. (
5
), with the harmonic and perturbative part of the action

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{01}

\displaystyle=\int\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}^{d}r\,}{\!\mathrm{d}^{d}r\,}{\!\mathrm{d}^{d}r\,}{\!\mathrm{d}^{d}r\,}\int\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}\Omega\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\Omega\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\Omega\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\Omega\,}\int\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}t\,}{\!\mathrm{d}t\,}{\!\mathrm{d}t\,}{\!\mathrm{d}t\,}\tilde{\chi}(\mathbf{r},\Omega,t)\big(-\partial_{t}+\mathcal{L}_{1}-r\big)\chi(\mathbf{r},\Omega,t)

(9a)

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{P1}

\displaystyle=\int\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}^{d}r\,}{\!\mathrm{d}^{d}r\,}{\!\mathrm{d}^{d}r\,}{\!\mathrm{d}^{d}r\,}\int\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}\Omega\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\Omega\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\Omega\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\Omega\,}\int\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}t\,}{\!\mathrm{d}t\,}{\!\mathrm{d}t\,}{\!\mathrm{d}t\,}\tilde{\chi}(\mathbf{r},\Omega,t)\nabla_{\mathbf{r}}\cdot\big(\nabla_{\mathbf{r}}\Upsilon(\mathbf{r})\big)\chi(\mathbf{r},\Omega,t)\ ,

(9b)

which is more efficiently dealt with using Gauss’ theorem in
\mathcal{A}_{P1}
to make
\nabla_{\mathbf{r}}
act on
\tilde{\chi}
rather than
\Upsilon^{\prime}\chi
.

The benefit of
including the self-propulsion in the harmonic part of the action
\mathcal{A}_{01}
, Eq. (
9a
), via
\mathcal{L}_{1}
, Eq. (
5a
), at the expense of having to find suitable eigenfunctions,
is to have only one perturbative vertex to worry about, namely that representing the external potential. The major disadvantage is that the diagonalising effect of the eigenfunctions on
\mathcal{L}_{1}
relies on momentum conservation, which is notably broken in the presence of an external potential, so that the external potential becomes significantly more difficult to handle. A similar problem would be encountered if we were to allow for pair interaction, in which case the action contains terms that are not bilinear, which result in difficult integrals involving products of more than two eigenfunctions.

The eigenfunctions of the angular part of
\mathcal{L}_{1}
in two spatial dimensions, after some manipulation, are the
\pi
-periodic Mathieu functions
ZIENER2012
, as discussed further below. Compared to other orthonormal systems, they are far less well studied
abramowitzStegun
. In three dimensions, we shall call them ”three-dimensional Mathieu functions”, but as far as we are aware, they have not been introduced in the literature of orthonormal functions in their own right. We have characterised them to some extent in App.
A
using similar tools as outlined in
ZIENER2012
. Of course, the complete framework of Sturm-Liouville theory provides broad theoretical backing. Once their existence is established, the eigenfunctions need to be determined only to the extent that the observables require it.

In the second scheme, the fields are written in terms of eigenfunctions of the simpler
\mathcal{L}_{2}
in Eq. (
5
), with the harmonic and perturbative part of the action

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{02}

\displaystyle=\int\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}^{d}r\,}{\!\mathrm{d}^{d}r\,}{\!\mathrm{d}^{d}r\,}{\!\mathrm{d}^{d}r\,}\int\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}\Omega\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\Omega\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\Omega\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\Omega\,}\int\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}t\,}{\!\mathrm{d}t\,}{\!\mathrm{d}t\,}{\!\mathrm{d}t\,}\tilde{\chi}(\mathbf{r},\Omega,t)\big(-\partial_{t}+\mathcal{L}_{2}-r\big)\chi(\mathbf{r},\Omega,t)

(10a)

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{P2}

\displaystyle=\int\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}^{d}r\,}{\!\mathrm{d}^{d}r\,}{\!\mathrm{d}^{d}r\,}{\!\mathrm{d}^{d}r\,}\int\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}\Omega\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\Omega\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\Omega\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\Omega\,}\int\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}t\,}{\!\mathrm{d}t\,}{\!\mathrm{d}t\,}{\!\mathrm{d}t\,}\tilde{\chi}(\mathbf{r},\Omega,t)\nabla_{\mathbf{r}}\cdot\big(\nabla_{\mathbf{r}}\Upsilon(\mathbf{r})-\mathbf{w}(\Omega)\big)\chi(\mathbf{r},\Omega,t).

(10b)

This results in simpler expressions overall, but suffers from the significant disadvantage of an additional vertex mediating the self-propulsion. One may expect a significantly enlarged number of diagrams to be considered in any interesting observable, but this is not the case for the observables considered here.

We proceed by determining the MSD and the stationary density for ABPs in two dimensions. In the course, we will discuss the details of the action, the diagrammatics and the eigenfunctions.

II
Active Brownian Particles in
2
dimensions

II.1
Mathieu functions

The starting point of the present derivation is
the operator
\mathcal{L}_{1}
in Eq. (
5a
)
and
the action Eq. (
9a
), restated here with the explicit parameterisation of the director

\mathcal{A}_{01}[\tilde{\chi},\chi]=\int\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}^{2}r\,}{\!\mathrm{d}^{2}r\,}{\!\mathrm{d}^{2}r\,}{\!\mathrm{d}^{2}r\,}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}\varphi\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\varphi\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\varphi\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\varphi\,}\int\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}t\,}{\!\mathrm{d}t\,}{\!\mathrm{d}t\,}{\!\mathrm{d}t\,}\tilde{\chi}(\mathbf{r},\varphi,t)\big(-\partial_{t}+\mathcal{L}_{1}-r\big)\chi(\mathbf{r},\varphi,t)

(11)

for convenience. The key difficulty here is to find eigenfunctions of the operator
-\mathbf{w}\cdot\nabla_{\mathbf{r}}+\nabla_{\Omega}^{2}
. This is greatly helped by measuring the azimuthal angle relative to the reciprocal vector
\mathbf{k}
, as we will discuss in the following. We first introduce

\displaystyle\chi(\mathbf{r},\varphi,t)

\displaystyle=\int\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega\,}\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22^{2}k\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22^{2}k\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22^{2}k\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22^{2}k\,}\sum_{\ell=0}^{\infty}\mathchoice{\mathrm{e}^{-\mathring{\imath}\omega t}}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-\mathring{\imath}\omega t\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-\mathring{\imath}\omega t\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-\mathring{\imath}\omega t\right)}\mathchoice{\mathrm{e}^{\mathring{\imath}\mathbf{k}\cdot\mathbf{r}}}{\operatorname{exp}\left(\mathring{\imath}\mathbf{k}\cdot\mathbf{r}\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(\mathring{\imath}\mathbf{k}\cdot\mathbf{r}\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(\mathring{\imath}\mathbf{k}\cdot\mathbf{r}\right)}u_{\ell}\left(\frac{\varphi-\sigma(\mathbf{k})}{2},q(|\mathbf{k}|)\right)\chi_{\ell}(\mathbf{k},\omega)

(12a)

\displaystyle\tilde{\chi}(\mathbf{r},\varphi,t)

\displaystyle=\int\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega\,}\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22^{2}k\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22^{2}k\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22^{2}k\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22^{2}k\,}\sum_{\ell=0}^{\infty}\mathchoice{\mathrm{e}^{-\mathring{\imath}\omega t}}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-\mathring{\imath}\omega t\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-\mathring{\imath}\omega t\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-\mathring{\imath}\omega t\right)}\mathchoice{\mathrm{e}^{\mathring{\imath}\mathbf{k}\cdot\mathbf{r}}}{\operatorname{exp}\left(\mathring{\imath}\mathbf{k}\cdot\mathbf{r}\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(\mathring{\imath}\mathbf{k}\cdot\mathbf{r}\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(\mathring{\imath}\mathbf{k}\cdot\mathbf{r}\right)}\widetilde{u}_{\ell}\left(\frac{\varphi-\sigma(-\mathbf{k})}{2},q(|-\mathbf{k}|)\right)\tilde{\chi}_{\ell}(\mathbf{k},\omega)

(12b)

where

u_{\ell}\left(\gamma,q(k)\right)

and

\widetilde{u}_{\ell}\left(\gamma,q(k)\right)

are suitably normalised
\pi
-periodic Mathieu functions

ZIENER2012
;
abramowitzStegun
, depending on the dimensionless, purely imaginary parameter

q(k)=\frac{2\mathring{\imath}w_{0}k}{\mathsf{D}_{r}}

(13)

which is a function of the (absolute) magnitude
k=|\mathbf{k}|=|-\mathbf{k}|
of the
\mathbf{k}
vector.
The function
\sigma(\mathbf{k})
, hitherto undetermined, allows a crucial simplification of the action further below. For the time being it suffices to state orthogonality even in the presence of the shift by
\sigma
,

\delta\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22(\mathbf{k}+\mathbf{k}^{\prime})\int_{0}^{2\pi}\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}\varphi\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\varphi\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\varphi\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\varphi\,}u_{\ell}\left(\frac{\varphi-\sigma(\mathbf{k})}{2},q(|\mathbf{k}|)\right)\widetilde{u}_{\ell^{\prime}}\left(\frac{\varphi-\sigma(-\mathbf{k}^{\prime})}{2},q(|-\mathbf{k}^{\prime}|)\right)=\delta\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22(\mathbf{k}+\mathbf{k}^{\prime})\delta_{\ell,\ell^{\prime}}

(14)

as the
\delta\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22(\mathbf{k}+\mathbf{k}^{\prime})
forces this shift to be identical,
\sigma(\mathbf{k})=\sigma(-\mathbf{k}^{\prime})
, which can therefore be absorbed into the dummy variable and subsequently into a change of the integration limits, from where it disappears, because of the periodicity of the integrand.

Using Eq. (
12
) in the action Eq. (
11
) produces

\mathcal{A}_{01}[\tilde{\chi},\chi]=-\int\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22^{2}k\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22^{2}k\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22^{2}k\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22^{2}k\,}\sum_{\ell,\ell^{\prime}}\int\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega\,}\tilde{\chi}_{\ell^{\prime}}(-\mathbf{k},-\omega)\chi_{\ell}(\mathbf{k},\omega)\\
\times\int_{0}^{2\pi}\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}\varphi\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\varphi\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\varphi\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\varphi\,}\tilde{u}_{\ell^{\prime}}\left(\frac{\varphi-\sigma(\mathbf{k})}{2},q(k)\right)\big(-\mathring{\imath}\omega+\mathsf{D}_{t}\mathbf{k}^{2}+\mathring{\imath}w_{0}k\cos(\varphi-\alpha)-\mathsf{D}_{r}\partial_{\varphi}^{2}\big)u_{\ell}\left(\frac{\varphi-\sigma(\mathbf{k})}{2},q(k)\right)\ ,

(15)

where
\alpha
is the polar angle of
\mathbf{k}=k(\cos\alpha,\sin\alpha)^{\mathsf{T}}
, so that
\mathbf{k}\cdot\mathbf{w}(\varphi)=kw(\cos\alpha\cos\varphi+\sin\alpha\sin\varphi)=kw\cos(\varphi-\alpha)
.
Choosing
\sigma(\mathbf{k})=\alpha
allows a change of variables and a change of integration limits, so that the last integral in Eq. (
15
) reads

\displaystyle\phantom{=}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}\varphi\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\varphi\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\varphi\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\varphi\,}\tilde{u}_{\ell^{\prime}}\left(\frac{\varphi-\sigma(\mathbf{k})}{2},q(k)\right)\bigg(-\mathring{\imath}\omega+\mathsf{D}_{t}\mathbf{k}^{2}+\mathring{\imath}w_{0}k\cos(\varphi-\alpha)-\mathsf{D}_{r}\partial_{\varphi}^{2}+r\bigg)u_{\ell}\left(\frac{\varphi-\sigma(\mathbf{k})}{2},q(k)\right)

(16a)

\displaystyle=2\int_{0}^{\pi}\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}\gamma\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\gamma\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\gamma\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\gamma\,}\tilde{u}_{\ell^{\prime}}\left(\gamma,q\right)\bigg(-\mathring{\imath}\omega+\mathsf{D}_{t}\mathbf{k}^{2}+\mathchoice{\frac{1}{4}}{(1/4)}{\frac{1}{4}}{(1/4)}\mathsf{D}_{r}(2q\cos(2\gamma)-\partial_{\gamma}^{2})+r\bigg)u_{\ell}\left(\gamma,q\right)

(16b)

\displaystyle=\delta_{\ell^{\prime},\ell}\bigg(-\mathring{\imath}\omega+\mathsf{D}_{t}\mathbf{k}^{2}+\mathchoice{\frac{1}{4}}{(1/4)}{\frac{1}{4}}{(1/4)}\mathsf{D}_{r}\lambda_{\ell}(q)+r\bigg)

(16c)

where in the last equality we have used
that the Mathieu functions obey

\left(\partial_{\gamma}^{2}-2q\cos(2\gamma)\right)u_{\ell}(\gamma,q)=-\lambda_{\ell}(q)u_{\ell}(\gamma,q)

(17)

and the orthonormality Eq. (
14
).

With this simplification the action Eq. (
11
) becomes diagonal

\mathcal{A}_{01}[\tilde{\chi},\chi]=-\int\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22^{2}k\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22^{2}k\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22^{2}k\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22^{2}k\,}\sum_{\ell}\int\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega\,}\tilde{\chi}_{\ell}(-\mathbf{k},-\omega)\chi_{\ell}(\mathbf{k},\omega)\bigg(-\mathring{\imath}\omega+\mathsf{D}_{t}\mathbf{k}^{2}+\mathsf{D}_{r}\lambda_{\ell}(q(k))/4+r\bigg)\ ,

(18)

and the (bare) propagator can immediately be read off

\displaystyle\left\langle\chi_{\ell}(\mathbf{k},\omega)\tilde{\chi}_{\ell_{0}}(\mathbf{k}_{0},\omega_{0})\right\rangle_{0}=\frac{\delta_{\ell,\ell_{0}}\delta\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22(\mathbf{k}+\mathbf{k}_{0})\delta\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22(\omega+\omega_{0})}{-\mathring{\imath}\omega+\mathsf{D}_{t}\mathbf{k}^{2}+\mathsf{D}_{r}\lambda_{\ell}(q(k))/4+r}=\delta_{\ell,\ell_{0}}\delta\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22(\mathbf{k}+\mathbf{k}_{0})\delta\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22(\omega+\omega_{0})G_{\ell}\left(\mathbf{k},\omega\right)

(19a)

\displaystyle\triangleq\hbox to73.42pt{\vbox to31.87pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 34.46446pt\lower-15.71045pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\ ,

(19b)

where we have introduced
G_{\ell}\left(\mathbf{k},\omega\right)
as a shorthand, as well as the diagrammatic representation to be used in Section
II.1.2
.

II.1.1
Mean squared displacement

The mean-squared displacement (MSD)
\overline{\mathbf{r}^{2}}(t)
of a two-dimensional ABP (2DABP) can be calculated on the basis of a few algebraic properties of the
u_{\ell}(\gamma,q)
. The probability of finding a 2DABP with director
\varphi
at position
\mathbf{r}
at time
t
, after being placed at
\mathbf{r}_{0}
with director
\varphi_{0}
at time
t_{0}
is
\left\langle\chi(\mathbf{r},\varphi,t)\tilde{\chi}(\mathbf{r}_{0},\varphi_{0},t_{0})\right\rangle
, which is easily determined from Eq. (
19
) as

\left\langle\chi(\mathbf{k},\varphi,t)\tilde{\chi}(\mathbf{k}_{0},\varphi_{0},t_{0})\right\rangle=\delta\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22(\mathbf{k}+\mathbf{k}_{0})\sum_{\ell=0}^{\infty}u_{\ell}\left(\frac{\varphi-\sigma(\mathbf{k})}{2},q(k)\right)\tilde{u}_{\ell}\left(\frac{\varphi_{0}-\sigma(\mathbf{k})}{2},q(k)\right)\mathchoice{\mathrm{e}^{-(\mathsf{D}_{t}k^{2}+\mathchoice{\frac{1}{4}}{(1/4)}{\frac{1}{4}}{(1/4)}\mathsf{D}_{r}\lambda_{\ell}(q(k))t}}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-(\mathsf{D}_{t}k^{2}+\mathchoice{\frac{1}{4}}{(1/4)}{\frac{1}{4}}{(1/4)}\mathsf{D}_{r}\lambda_{\ell}(q(k))t\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-(\mathsf{D}_{t}k^{2}+\mathchoice{\frac{1}{4}}{(1/4)}{\frac{1}{4}}{(1/4)}\mathsf{D}_{r}\lambda_{\ell}(q(k))t\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-(\mathsf{D}_{t}k^{2}+\mathchoice{\frac{1}{4}}{(1/4)}{\frac{1}{4}}{(1/4)}\mathsf{D}_{r}\lambda_{\ell}(q(k))t\right)}

(20)

up to an inverse of Fourier-transform of
\mathbf{k}
.
Making use of the Fourier transform, the MSD of a 2DABP irrespective of the final director is

\overline{\mathbf{r}^{2}}(t)=-\left.\nabla_{\mathbf{k}}^{2}\right|_{\mathbf{k}=\mathbf{0}}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}\varphi\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\varphi\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\varphi\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\varphi\,}\sum_{\ell=0}^{\infty}u_{\ell}\left(\frac{\varphi-\sigma(\mathbf{k})}{2},q(k)\right)\tilde{u}_{\ell}\left(\frac{\varphi_{0}-\sigma(\mathbf{k})}{2},q(k)\right)\mathchoice{\mathrm{e}^{-(\mathsf{D}_{t}k^{2}+\mathchoice{\frac{1}{4}}{(1/4)}{\frac{1}{4}}{(1/4)}\mathsf{D}_{r}\lambda_{\ell}(q(k))t}}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-(\mathsf{D}_{t}k^{2}+\mathchoice{\frac{1}{4}}{(1/4)}{\frac{1}{4}}{(1/4)}\mathsf{D}_{r}\lambda_{\ell}(q(k))t\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-(\mathsf{D}_{t}k^{2}+\mathchoice{\frac{1}{4}}{(1/4)}{\frac{1}{4}}{(1/4)}\mathsf{D}_{r}\lambda_{\ell}(q(k))t\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-(\mathsf{D}_{t}k^{2}+\mathchoice{\frac{1}{4}}{(1/4)}{\frac{1}{4}}{(1/4)}\mathsf{D}_{r}\lambda_{\ell}(q(k))t\right)}

(21)

Having integrated over
\varphi
, it is obvious that the MSD cannot depend on the initial orientation of the director,
\varphi_{0}
, as changing it, amounts to a rotation of the isotropic plane. This is indeed trivial to show when
w_{0}=0
, in which case
q(k)=0
and the Mathieu functions degenerate into trigonometric functions. However, demonstrating this when
w_{0}\neq 0
is far from trivial.
We proceed by rewriting the MSD as an average

\int\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}\varphi_{0}\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\varphi_{0}\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\varphi_{0}\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\varphi_{0}\,}(2\pi)^{-1}

of Eq. (
21
) over the initial angle
\varphi_{0}
,
so that both Mathieu functions,
u_{\ell}
and
\tilde{u}_{\ell}
reduce to a single coefficient via

\int_{0}^{2\pi}\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}\varphi\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\varphi\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\varphi\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\varphi\,}u_{\ell}(\varphi/2,q(k))=2\pi A_{\ell,0}(q(k))\ ,

(22)

which vanishes in fact for all odd indices
\ell
. We thus arrive at

\displaystyle\overline{\mathbf{r}^{2}}(t)

\displaystyle=-\left.\nabla_{\mathbf{k}}^{2}\right|_{\mathbf{k}=\mathbf{0}}2\mathchoice{\mathrm{e}^{-\mathsf{D}_{t}k^{2}t}}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-\mathsf{D}_{t}k^{2}t\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-\mathsf{D}_{t}k^{2}t\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-\mathsf{D}_{t}k^{2}t\right)}\sum_{\ell=0}^{\infty}\left(A_{\ell,0}(q(k))\right)^{2}\mathchoice{\mathrm{e}^{-\mathchoice{\frac{1}{4}}{(1/4)}{\frac{1}{4}}{(1/4)}\mathsf{D}_{r}\lambda_{\ell}(q(k)t}}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-\mathchoice{\frac{1}{4}}{(1/4)}{\frac{1}{4}}{(1/4)}\mathsf{D}_{r}\lambda_{\ell}(q(k)t\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-\mathchoice{\frac{1}{4}}{(1/4)}{\frac{1}{4}}{(1/4)}\mathsf{D}_{r}\lambda_{\ell}(q(k)t\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-\mathchoice{\frac{1}{4}}{(1/4)}{\frac{1}{4}}{(1/4)}\mathsf{D}_{r}\lambda_{\ell}(q(k)t\right)}

(23)

The
u_{\ell}(\gamma,q)
are in fact the even
\pi
-periodic Mathieu functions for even
\ell
and odd
\pi
-periodic Mathieu functions for odd
\ell

ZIENER2012
.
The
\widetilde{u}_{\ell}\left(\gamma,q\right)
differ from the
u_{\ell}\left(\gamma,q\right)
only by a factor
1/\pi

ZhangPruessner:2022
,

\widetilde{u}_{\ell}\left(\gamma,q\right)=\pi^{-1}u_{\ell}\left(\gamma,q\right)\ ,

(24)

which is a convenient way to meet the orthogonality relation Eq. (
14
) while maintaining that the non-tilde
u_{\ell}\left(\gamma,q\right)
are standard Mathieu functions.
At this stage, all we need to know about the Mathieu functions is
ZIENER2012

\displaystyle A_{0,0}(q)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}-\frac{q^{2}}{16\sqrt{2}}+\mathcal{O}\!\left(q^{3}\right)

(25a)

\displaystyle A_{2,0}(q)

\displaystyle=\frac{q}{4}+\mathcal{O}\!\left(q^{2}\right)

(25b)

\displaystyle A_{2j,0}(q)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}\!\left(q^{j}\right)

(25c)

\displaystyle A_{2j+1,0}(q)

\displaystyle=0

(25d)

\displaystyle\lambda_{0}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}\!\left(q^{2}\right)

(25e)

\displaystyle\lambda_{2}

\displaystyle=4+\mathcal{O}\!\left(q^{2}\right)

(25f)

for
j\in\mathds{N}_{0}
, which means that only two of the
A_{\ell,0}
in the sum Eq. (
23
) contribute, namely
\ell=0
and
\ell=2
, as all other
A_{\ell,0}
vanish at
q(k=0)=0
even when differentiated twice. With Eq. (
25
) the MSD becomes

ZhangPruessner:2022

\overline{\mathbf{r}^{2}}(t)=4\mathsf{D}_{t}t+2\frac{w_{0}^{2}}{\mathsf{D}_{r}^{2}}\left(e^{-\mathsf{D}_{r}t}-1+\mathsf{D}_{r}t\right)\ ,

(26)

identical to that of Run-and-Tumble particles, Eq. (49) of
ZhangPruessner:2022
, if the tumble rate
\alpha
is
\mathsf{D}_{r}
.
In asymptotically large
t
, the MSD of a 2DABP is equivalent to that of conventional diffusion with effective diffusion constant

\mathsf{D}^{\text{2D}}_{\text{eff}}=\mathsf{D}_{t}+\frac{w_{0}^{2}}{2\mathsf{D}_{r}}\ ,

(27)

and RnT motion with tumbling rate

\alpha=\mathsf{D}_{r}

Cates:2013
;
Cates:2015
;
ZhangPruessner:2022
.

We will use the above as a template for the derivation of the MSD of 3DABP.
There, we will make use again of the isotropy of the initial condition
\varphi_{0}
and further of the isotropy of the displacement in any of the three spatial directions. In fact, picking a ”preferred direction” can greatly reduce the difficulty of the present calculation.

II.1.2
External Potential

Next, we place the 2DABP in a (two-dimensional) potential
\Upsilon(\mathbf{r})
. Without self-propulsion, the density
\rho_{0}(\mathbf{r})
at stationarity is given by the barometric formula
\rho_{0}(\mathbf{r})\propto\mathchoice{\mathrm{e}^{-\Upsilon(\mathbf{r})/\mathsf{D}_{t}}}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-\Upsilon(\mathbf{r})/\mathsf{D}_{t}\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-\Upsilon(\mathbf{r})/\mathsf{D}_{t}\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-\Upsilon(\mathbf{r})/\mathsf{D}_{t}\right)}
. As a sanity check for the present field-theoretic framework, we derive it in App.
C
. In the present section we want to determine the effect of the self-propulsion, which we expect is partially covered by
\mathsf{D}_{t}
in the Boltzmann distribution being replaced by
\mathsf{D}^{\text{2D}}_{\text{eff}}
, Eq. (
27
).

As the external potential is treated perturbatively, stationarity even at vanishing potential requires a finite volume, which we assume is a periodic square,
i.e.
a torus, with linear extent
L
. This requires a small adjustment of the Fourier representation introduced in Eq. (
12
), which now reads

\displaystyle\chi(\mathbf{r},\varphi,t)

\displaystyle=\int\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega\,}\frac{1}{L^{2}}\sum_{\mathbf{n}\in\mathds{Z}^{2}}\sum_{\ell=0}^{\infty}\mathchoice{\mathrm{e}^{-\mathring{\imath}\omega t}}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-\mathring{\imath}\omega t\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-\mathring{\imath}\omega t\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-\mathring{\imath}\omega t\right)}\mathchoice{\mathrm{e}^{\mathring{\imath}\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}}\cdot\mathbf{r}}}{\operatorname{exp}\left(\mathring{\imath}\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}}\cdot\mathbf{r}\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(\mathring{\imath}\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}}\cdot\mathbf{r}\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(\mathring{\imath}\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}}\cdot\mathbf{r}\right)}u_{\ell}\left(\frac{\varphi-\sigma(\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}})}{2},q(|\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}}|)\right)\chi_{\ell}(\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}},\omega)

(28a)

\displaystyle\tilde{\chi}(\mathbf{r},\varphi,t)

\displaystyle=\int\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega\,}\frac{1}{L^{2}}\sum_{\mathbf{n}\in\mathds{Z}^{2}}\sum_{\ell=0}^{\infty}\mathchoice{\mathrm{e}^{-\mathring{\imath}\omega t}}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-\mathring{\imath}\omega t\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-\mathring{\imath}\omega t\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-\mathring{\imath}\omega t\right)}\mathchoice{\mathrm{e}^{\mathring{\imath}\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}}\cdot\mathbf{r}}}{\operatorname{exp}\left(\mathring{\imath}\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}}\cdot\mathbf{r}\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(\mathring{\imath}\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}}\cdot\mathbf{r}\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(\mathring{\imath}\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}}\cdot\mathbf{r}\right)}\widetilde{u}_{\ell}\left(\frac{\varphi-\sigma(-\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}})}{2},q(|-\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}}|)\right)\tilde{\chi}_{\ell}(\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}},\omega)

(28b)

where
L^{-2}\sum_{\mathbf{n}\in\mathds{Z}^{2}}
has replaced the integration over
\mathbf{k}
in Eq. (
12
), with the couple
\mathbf{n}=(n_{x},n_{y})^{\mathsf{T}}
running over the entire
\mathds{Z}^{2}
and
\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}}=\mathbf{n}L/(2\pi)
. The bare propagator Eq. (
19
) is adjusted simply by replacing
\delta\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22(\mathbf{k}+\mathbf{k}_{0})
by, say,
L^{2}\delta_{\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{p},\mathbf{0}}
.
With the introduction of Fourier sums, we implement periodic boundary conditions, effectively implementing periodic observables and, below, a periodic potential.

As far as the Mathieu functions are concerned this adjustment to a finite domain has no significant consequences.
Due to the Mathieu functions, however, the perturbative part of the action
\mathcal{A}_{P1}
Eq. (
9b
) gets significantly more difficult, as the functions are orthogonal only if the
\mathbf{k}
-modes match,

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{P1}

\displaystyle=\int\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}^{d}r\,}{\!\mathrm{d}^{d}r\,}{\!\mathrm{d}^{d}r\,}{\!\mathrm{d}^{d}r\,}\int\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}\Omega\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\Omega\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\Omega\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\Omega\,}\int\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}t\,}{\!\mathrm{d}t\,}{\!\mathrm{d}t\,}{\!\mathrm{d}t\,}\tilde{\chi}(\mathbf{r},\Omega,t)\nabla_{\mathbf{r}}\cdot\big(\nabla_{\mathbf{r}}\Upsilon(\mathbf{r})\big)\chi(\mathbf{r},\Omega,t)

(29a)

\displaystyle=\int\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega\,}\frac{1}{L^{6}}\sum_{\mathbf{n}_{1},\mathbf{n}_{2},\mathbf{n}_{3}}L^{2}\delta_{\mathbf{n}_{1}+\mathbf{n}_{2}+\mathbf{n}_{3},\mathbf{0}}\sum_{\ell_{1},\ell_{3}}(\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}_{1}}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}_{2}})\tilde{\chi}_{\ell_{1}}(\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}_{1}},-\omega)\Upsilon_{\mathbf{n}_{2}}\chi_{\ell_{3}}(\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}_{3}},\omega)

(29b)

\displaystyle\quad\times\int_{0}^{2\pi}\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}\varphi\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\varphi\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\varphi\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\varphi\,}u_{\ell_{3}}\left(\frac{\varphi-\sigma(\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}_{3}})}{2},q(|\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}_{3}}|)\right)\widetilde{u}_{\ell_{1}}\left(\frac{\varphi-\sigma(-\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}_{1}})}{2},q(|\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}_{1}}|)\right)

(29c)

where each
\mathbf{n}_{1}
,
\mathbf{n}_{2}
and
\mathbf{n}_{3}
runs over all
\mathds{Z}^{2}
in the sum and the Kronecker
\delta
-function
\delta_{\mathbf{n}_{1}+\mathbf{n}_{2}+\mathbf{n}_{3},\mathbf{0}}
enforces
\mathbf{n}_{1}+\mathbf{n}_{2}+\mathbf{n}_{3}=\mathbf{0}
. Similarly,
\ell_{1}
and
\ell_{3}
run over all non-negative integers indexing the Mathieu functions. The potential
\Upsilon(\mathbf{r})
now enters via its modes
\Upsilon_{\mathbf{n}}
, more explictly

\displaystyle\Upsilon(\mathbf{r})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{L^{2}}\sum_{\mathbf{n}\in\mathds{Z}^{2}}\Upsilon_{\mathbf{n}}\mathchoice{\mathrm{e}^{\mathring{\imath}\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}}\cdot\mathbf{r}}}{\operatorname{exp}\left(\mathring{\imath}\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}}\cdot\mathbf{r}\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(\mathring{\imath}\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}}\cdot\mathbf{r}\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(\mathring{\imath}\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}}\cdot\mathbf{r}\right)}

(30a)

\displaystyle\Upsilon_{\mathbf{n}}

\displaystyle=\iint\limits_{0}^{\hskip 8.19447ptL}\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}^{2}r\,}{\!\mathrm{d}^{2}r\,}{\!\mathrm{d}^{2}r\,}{\!\mathrm{d}^{2}r\,}\Upsilon(\mathbf{r})\mathchoice{\mathrm{e}^{-\mathring{\imath}\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}}\cdot\mathbf{r}}}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-\mathring{\imath}\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}}\cdot\mathbf{r}\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-\mathring{\imath}\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}}\cdot\mathbf{r}\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-\mathring{\imath}\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}}\cdot\mathbf{r}\right)}\ ,

(30b)

rendering the potential effectively periodic.

Of course, a non-trivial
\Upsilon(\mathbf{r})
spoils translational invariance and correspondingly
\Upsilon_{\mathbf{n}}
provides a
source
of momentum in Eq. (
29b
), so that
\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}_{3}}
is generally not equal to
-\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}_{1}}
, with the sole exception of
\mathbf{n}_{2}=\mathbf{0}
. As a result, generally
\sigma(\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}_{3}})\neq\sigma(-\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}_{1}})

and

q(|\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}_{3}}|)\neq q(|\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}_{1}}|)
, Eq. (
13
). The integral

\Delta_{\ell_{1},\ell_{3}}(-\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}_{1}},-\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}_{3}})=\int_{0}^{2\pi}\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}\varphi\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\varphi\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\varphi\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\varphi\,}u_{\ell_{3}}\left(\frac{\varphi-\sigma(\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}_{3}})}{2},q(|\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}_{3}}|)\right)\widetilde{u}_{\ell_{1}}\left(\frac{\varphi-\sigma(-\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}_{1}})}{2},q(|\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}_{1}}|)\right)

(31)

however, is generally diagonal in
\ell_{1},\ell_{3}
only when
\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}_{1}}=-\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}_{3}}
. It is this projection, Eq. (
31
), that is at the heart of the complications to come. It is notably absent in the case of purely diffusive particles, App.
C.4
. With the help of Eq. (
31
), the perturbative action Eq. (
29b
) can be written as

\mathcal{A}_{P1}=\int\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega\,}\frac{1}{L^{6}}\sum_{\mathbf{n}_{1},\mathbf{n}_{2},\mathbf{n}_{3}}L^{2}\delta_{\mathbf{n}_{1}+\mathbf{n}_{2}+\mathbf{n}_{3},\mathbf{0}}\sum_{\ell_{1},\ell_{3}}(\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}_{1}}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}_{2}})\tilde{\chi}_{\ell_{1}}(\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}_{1}},-\omega)\Upsilon_{\mathbf{n}_{2}}\chi_{\ell_{3}}(\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}_{3}},\omega)\Delta_{\ell_{1},\ell_{3}}(-\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}_{1}},-\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}_{3}})\ .

(32)

The perturbative part of the action, Eq. (
29b
), may diagrammatically be written as

where a dash across any line serves as a reminder that the vertex carries a factor of

. The two factors of
\mathbf{k}
are multiplied in an inner product. The dangling bauble in Eq. (
33
) represents the external potential, which is a source of momentum.

We will determine in the following the stationary density

\rho_{0}(\mathbf{r})=\lim_{t_{0}\to-\infty}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}\varphi\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\varphi\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\varphi\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\varphi\,}\left\langle\chi(\mathbf{r},\varphi,t)\tilde{\chi}(\mathbf{r}_{0},\varphi_{0},t_{0})\right\rangle

at position
\mathbf{r}
, starting with a single particle placed at
\mathbf{r}_{0}
with orientation
\varphi_{0}
at time
t_{0}
. We will determine this density
to first order in the potential and second order in

, Eq. (

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}+\ldots\ ,

(35)

where the first diagram represents the bare propagator as introduced in Eq. (
19
) and the second diagram the contribution to first order due to the external potential Eq. (
33
). The limit
t_{0}\to-\infty
needs to be taken after an inverse Fourier transform in
\omega
and
\omega_{0}
. The structure of the propagator Eq. (
19
), essentially of the form
(-\mathring{\imath}\omega+\lambda+r)^{-1}
with some non-negative
\lambda\geq 0
dependent on the spatial and angular modes, means that every such Fourier transform results in an expression proportional to
\mathchoice{\mathrm{e}^{-(\lambda+r)(t-t_{0})}}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-(\lambda+r)(t-t_{0})\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-(\lambda+r)(t-t_{0})\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-(\lambda+r)(t-t_{0})\right)}
. Removing the mass by
r\downarrow 0
and taking
t_{0}\to-\infty
results in the expression vanishing, except when
\Re(\lambda)\leq 0

Zhen:2022
, which is the case, by inspection of Eq. (
19
), only when
\mathbf{k}=\mathbf{0}
and
\Re{\lambda_{\ell}(q(\mathbf{k}))}\leq 0
, which requires
\ell=0
, as

\displaystyle\lambda_{2\ell}(q)

\displaystyle=4\ell^{2}+\mathcal{O}\!\left(q\right)\ ,

(36a)

\displaystyle\lambda_{2\ell+1}(q)

\displaystyle=4(\ell+1)^{2}+\mathcal{O}\!\left(q\right)\ .

(36b)

Eq. (
36
) is easily verified as the periodic solutions of the Mathieu Eq. (
17
) are trigonometric functions. Any propagator carrying a factor of
\mathbf{k}
therefore vanishes under this operation. The only pole of the propagator to be considered is the one at
\omega=-\mathring{\imath}r
. Because only the rightmost, incoming leg in any of the diagrams is undashed, taking the inverse Fourier transform followed by the limit
t_{0}\to-\infty
thus amounts to replacing the incoming legs by
\delta_{\mathbf{n},0}
and every occurance of
\omega
in the diagram by
0
. This is equivalent to an amputation of the incoming leg
Zhen:2022
. At this point, the inverse Fourier transforms from
\mathbf{k}
to
\mathbf{r}
and the transforms of azimuthal degree of freedom from
\ell
to
\varphi
are still to be taken.

We may summarise by writing

{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} {}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -3.94 -7.87 L 3.94 -7.87}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} {}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 -23.62 M 3.94 -23.62 C 3.94 -21.45 2.17 -19.69 0 -19.69 C -2.17 -19.69 -3.94 -21.45 -3.94 -23.62 C -3.94 -25.8 -2.17 -27.56 0 -27.56 C 2.17 -27.56 3.94 -25.8 3.94 -23.62 Z M 0 -23.62}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\times\Big\{\delta_{\ell_{0},0}\delta_{\mathbf{p},\mathbf{0}}\Big\}=(-\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{p}})\Upsilon_{\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{p}}G_{\ell}\left(\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}},0\right)\delta_{\ell_{0},0}\delta_{\mathbf{p},\mathbf{0}}\Delta_{\ell,\ell_{0}}(\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}},\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{p}})

(38)

The projection
\Delta_{\ell,\ell_{0}}(\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}},\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{p}})
needs to be known only for
\ell_{0}=0
and
\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{p}}=\mathbf{0}
, and because
u_{\ell=0}\left(\gamma,q=0\right)=1/\sqrt{2}
, Eqs. (
22
) and (
31
) give

\Delta_{\ell,0}(\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}},\mathbf{0})=\sqrt{2}A_{\ell,0}(q(|\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}}|))

(39)

To carry out the integral over
\varphi
in Eq. (
34
), we have to transform Eq. (
37
) from
\ell,\ell_{0}
to
\varphi,\varphi_{0}
using Eq. (
12
) first,

\lim_{t_{0}\to-\infty}\left\langle\chi(\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}},\varphi,t)\tilde{\chi}(\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{p}},\varphi_{0},t_{0})\right\rangle=u_{0}\left(\frac{\varphi-\sigma(\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}})}{2},q(|\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}}|)\right)\widetilde{u}_{0}\left(\frac{\varphi_{0}-\sigma(-\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{p}})}{2},q(|\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{p}}|)\right)L^{2}\delta_{\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{p},0}\delta_{\mathbf{p},\mathbf{0}}\\
+\sum_{\ell=0}^{\infty}(-\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{p}})\Upsilon_{\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{p}}G_{\ell}\left(\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}},0\right)\delta_{\mathbf{p},\mathbf{0}}\sqrt{2}A_{\ell,0}(q(|\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}}|))u_{\ell}\left(\frac{\varphi-\sigma(\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}})}{2},q(|\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}}|)\right)\widetilde{u}_{0}\left(\frac{\varphi_{0}-\sigma(-\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{p}})}{2},q(|\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{p}}|)\right)+\ldots\ .

(40)

In the first term,
\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}}=\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{p}}=\mathbf{0}
, so that the product of the two Mathieu functions degenerates to
1/(2\pi)
, cancelling with the integration over
\varphi
still to be performed. In the second term, integration over
\varphi
can again be done with the help of Eq. (
22
), while the final Mathieu function
\widetilde{u}_{0}\left(\frac{\varphi_{0}-\sigma(-\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{p}})}{2},q(|\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{p}}|)\right)
is in fact
1/(\sqrt{2}\pi)
, once
\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{p}}=\mathbf{0}
is taken into account,

\int_{0}^{2\pi}\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}\varphi\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\varphi\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\varphi\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\varphi\,}\lim_{t_{0}\to-\infty}\left\langle\chi(\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}},\varphi,t)\tilde{\chi}(\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{p}},\varphi_{0},t_{0})\right\rangle=L^{2}\delta_{\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{p},0}\delta_{\mathbf{p},\mathbf{0}}+\sum_{\ell=0}^{\infty}(-\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{p}})\Upsilon_{\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{p}}G_{\ell}\left(\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}},0\right)\delta_{\mathbf{p},\mathbf{0}}2\big(A_{\ell,0}(q(|\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}}|))\big)^{2}+\ldots\ .

(41)

Since
A_{\ell,0}(q)
, Eq. (
25
), vanish for odd
\ell
and otherwise behave to leading order like
q^{\ell/2}
, Eq. (
25
), expanding these coefficients to second order means to retain only
\ell=0,2
in the sum, so that with explicit
G_{\ell}\left(\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}},0\right)
, Eq. (
19
), we arrive at

\int_{0}^{2\pi}\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}\varphi\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\varphi\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\varphi\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\varphi\,}\lim_{t_{0}\to-\infty}\left\langle\chi(\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}},\varphi,t)\tilde{\chi}(\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{p}},\varphi_{0},t_{0})\right\rangle=L^{2}\delta_{\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{p},0}\delta_{\mathbf{p},\mathbf{0}}+(-\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{p}})\Upsilon_{\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{p}}\delta_{\ell_{0},0}\delta_{\mathbf{p},\mathbf{0}}\\
\times\Big\{\big(\mathsf{D}_{t}k_{\mathbf{n}}^{2}+\mathsf{D}_{r}\lambda_{0}(q)/4+r\big)^{-1}\bigg(1-\frac{q^{2}}{16}\bigg)^{2}+\big(\mathsf{D}_{t}k_{\mathbf{n}}^{2}+\mathsf{D}_{r}\lambda_{2}(q)/4+r\big)^{-1}\frac{q^{2}}{8}\Big\}+\mathcal{O}\!\left(q^{4}\right)+\ldots\ ,

(42)

where
q^{2}=-4w_{0}^{2}k_{\mathbf{n}}^{2}\mathsf{D}_{r}^{-2}
and
k_{\mathbf{n}}=|\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}}|
. The limit
r\downarrow 0
is yet to be taken and
\ldots
on the right of Eq. (
42
) refers to the higher orders in the external potential.
Expanding the eigenvalues
\lambda_{\ell}
beyond Eq. (
25
),

\displaystyle\lambda_{0}

\displaystyle=-q^{2}/2+\mathcal{O}\!\left(q^{4}\right)=2w_{0}^{2}k_{\mathbf{n}}^{2}\mathsf{D}_{r}^{-2}+\mathcal{O}\!\left(q^{4}\right)

(43a)

\displaystyle\lambda_{2}

\displaystyle=4+\mathcal{O}\!\left(q^{2}\right)\ ,

(43b)

suggests that
\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{p}}\delta_{\mathbf{p},\mathbf{0}}
can be cancelled with
k_{\mathbf{n}}^{2}
in the denominator when
\ell=0
. However, for
\mathbf{n}=\mathbf{0}
the denominator does not vanish as
r>0
. Performing the inverse Fourier transform to return real space gives after
r\downarrow 0
,

\rho_{0}(\mathbf{r})=\frac{1}{L^{4}}\sum_{\mathbf{n},\mathbf{p}}\mathchoice{\mathrm{e}^{\mathring{\imath}(\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}}\cdot\mathbf{r}+\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{p}}\cdot\mathbf{r}_{0})}}{\operatorname{exp}\left(\mathring{\imath}(\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}}\cdot\mathbf{r}+\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{p}}\cdot\mathbf{r}_{0})\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(\mathring{\imath}(\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}}\cdot\mathbf{r}+\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{p}}\cdot\mathbf{r}_{0})\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(\mathring{\imath}(\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}}\cdot\mathbf{r}+\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{p}}\cdot\mathbf{r}_{0})\right)}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}\varphi\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\varphi\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\varphi\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\varphi\,}\lim_{t_{0}\to-\infty}\left\langle\chi(\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}},\varphi,t)\tilde{\chi}(\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{p}},\varphi_{0},t_{0})\right\rangle\\
=L^{-2}-\frac{1}{L^{4}}\sum_{\mathbf{n}\neq\mathbf{0}}\mathchoice{\mathrm{e}^{\mathring{\imath}\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}}\cdot\mathbf{r}}}{\operatorname{exp}\left(\mathring{\imath}\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}}\cdot\mathbf{r}\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(\mathring{\imath}\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}}\cdot\mathbf{r}\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(\mathring{\imath}\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}}\cdot\mathbf{r}\right)}\Upsilon_{\mathbf{n}}\Bigg\{\big(\mathsf{D}_{t}+w_{0}^{2}/(2\mathsf{D}_{r})+\mathcal{O}\!\left(q^{4}\right)/k_{\mathbf{n}}^{2}\big)^{-1}+\frac{k_{\mathbf{n}}^{4}w_{0}^{2}}{2\mathsf{D}_{r}^{2}}\Big[\big(\mathsf{D}_{t}k_{\mathbf{n}}^{2}+w_{0}^{2}k_{\mathbf{n}}^{2}/(2\mathsf{D}_{r})+\mathcal{O}\!\left(q^{4}\right)\big)^{-1}\\
-\big(\mathsf{D}_{t}k_{\mathbf{n}}^{2}+\mathsf{D}_{r}+\mathsf{D}_{r}\mathcal{O}\!\left(q^{2}\right)\big)^{-1}\Big]\Bigg\}+\mathcal{O}\!\left(q^{4}\right)+\ldots\ ,

(44)

where
\mathbf{n}=\mathbf{0}
is explicitly omitted from the sum and the first term in the curly brackets shows the expected
\mathsf{D}^{\text{2D}}_{\text{eff}}=\mathsf{D}_{t}+w_{0}^{2}/(2\mathsf{D}_{r})
, Eq. (
27
), amended, however, by a correction of order
q^{4}/k_{\mathbf{n}}^{2}
. To identify the lowest order correction in
k_{\mathbf{n}}
and weak potentials, we expand further than Eq. (
43
),

\lambda_{0}(q)=-\frac{q^{2}}{2}+\frac{7q^{4}}{128}+\mathcal{O}\!\left(q^{6}\right)=2w_{0}^{2}k_{\mathbf{n}}^{2}\mathsf{D}_{r}^{-2}+\frac{7}{8}w_{0}^{4}k_{\mathbf{n}}^{4}\mathsf{D}_{r}^{-4}+\mathcal{O}\!\left(q^{6}\right)

(45)

in the first (of three terms, in the curly bracket) correction term in the sum in Eq. (
44
),
which gives

\rho_{0}(\mathbf{r})=L^{-2}-\frac{1}{L^{4}}\sum_{\mathbf{n}\neq\mathbf{0}}\mathchoice{\mathrm{e}^{\mathring{\imath}\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}}\cdot\mathbf{r}}}{\operatorname{exp}\left(\mathring{\imath}\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}}\cdot\mathbf{r}\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(\mathring{\imath}\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}}\cdot\mathbf{r}\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(\mathring{\imath}\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}}\cdot\mathbf{r}\right)}\frac{\Upsilon_{\mathbf{n}}}{\mathsf{D}^{\text{2D}}_{\text{eff}}}\left\{1+\frac{w_{0}^{2}k_{\mathbf{n}}^{2}}{2\mathsf{D}_{r}^{2}}\bigg(1-\frac{7}{16}\frac{w_{0}^{2}}{\mathsf{D}_{r}\mathsf{D}^{\text{2D}}_{\text{eff}}}\bigg)+\mathcal{O}\!\left(k_{\mathbf{n}}^{4}\right)\right\}+\mathcal{O}\!\left(\Upsilon^{2}\right)

(46)

by dropping the last term in Eq. (
44
), as it is
\mathcal{O}\!\left(k_{\mathbf{n}}^{4}\right)
, and not expanding the second term any further, as it is
\mathcal{O}\!\left(k_{\mathbf{n}}^{2}\right)
as it stands.
The expression above it to be compared to the first order term in the barometric formula, Eq. (
162
), which produces only
-\Upsilon_{\mathbf{n}}/\mathsf{D}_{t}
as the first-order summand when setting
\mathbf{w}_{0}=\mathbf{0}
.
The corrections beyond that are generically due to the stochastic self-propulsion and amount to more than replacing
\mathsf{D}_{t}
by
\mathsf{D}^{\text{2D}}_{\text{eff}}
.
Eq. (
46
) completes the derivation in the present section. In the next section, we will re-derive MSD
\overline{\mathbf{r}^{2}}(t)
and density
\rho_{0}(\mathbf{r})
of active Brownian particles in two dimensions at stationarity, in an attempt to recover Eqs. (
26
) and (
44
) or (
46
) in a perturbation theory in small
w_{0}
and small potential.

II.2
Perturbation in small
w_{0}

The starting point of the present derivation is
the operator
\mathcal{L}_{2}
in Eq. (
5b
)
and
the harmonic part of the action Eq. (
10a
),

\mathcal{A}_{02}[\tilde{\chi},\chi]=\int\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}^{2}r\,}{\!\mathrm{d}^{2}r\,}{\!\mathrm{d}^{2}r\,}{\!\mathrm{d}^{2}r\,}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}\varphi\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\varphi\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\varphi\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\varphi\,}\int\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}t\,}{\!\mathrm{d}t\,}{\!\mathrm{d}t\,}{\!\mathrm{d}t\,}\tilde{\chi}(\mathbf{r},\varphi,t)\big(-\partial_{t}+\mathsf{D}_{t}\nabla_{\mathbf{r}}^{2}+\nabla^{2}_{\varphi}-r\big)\chi(\mathbf{r},\varphi,t)

(47)

which becomes diagonal with

\displaystyle\chi(\mathbf{r},\varphi,t)

\displaystyle=\int\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega\,}\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22^{2}k\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22^{2}k\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22^{2}k\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22^{2}k\,}\sum_{\ell=-\infty}^{\infty}\mathchoice{\mathrm{e}^{-\mathring{\imath}\omega t}}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-\mathring{\imath}\omega t\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-\mathring{\imath}\omega t\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-\mathring{\imath}\omega t\right)}\mathchoice{\mathrm{e}^{\mathring{\imath}\mathbf{k}\cdot\mathbf{r}}}{\operatorname{exp}\left(\mathring{\imath}\mathbf{k}\cdot\mathbf{r}\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(\mathring{\imath}\mathbf{k}\cdot\mathbf{r}\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(\mathring{\imath}\mathbf{k}\cdot\mathbf{r}\right)}\mathchoice{\mathrm{e}^{\mathring{\imath}\ell\varphi}}{\operatorname{exp}\left(\mathring{\imath}\ell\varphi\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(\mathring{\imath}\ell\varphi\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(\mathring{\imath}\ell\varphi\right)}\chi_{\ell}(\mathbf{k},\omega)

(48a)

\displaystyle\tilde{\chi}(\mathbf{r},\varphi,t)

\displaystyle=\int\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega\,}\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22^{2}k\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22^{2}k\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22^{2}k\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22^{2}k\,}\sum_{\ell=-\infty}^{\infty}\mathchoice{\mathrm{e}^{-\mathring{\imath}\omega t}}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-\mathring{\imath}\omega t\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-\mathring{\imath}\omega t\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-\mathring{\imath}\omega t\right)}\mathchoice{\mathrm{e}^{\mathring{\imath}\mathbf{k}\cdot\mathbf{r}}}{\operatorname{exp}\left(\mathring{\imath}\mathbf{k}\cdot\mathbf{r}\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(\mathring{\imath}\mathbf{k}\cdot\mathbf{r}\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(\mathring{\imath}\mathbf{k}\cdot\mathbf{r}\right)}\frac{\mathchoice{\mathrm{e}^{-\mathring{\imath}\ell\varphi}}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-\mathring{\imath}\ell\varphi\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-\mathring{\imath}\ell\varphi\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-\mathring{\imath}\ell\varphi\right)}}{2\pi}\tilde{\chi}_{\ell}(\mathbf{k},\omega)\ .

(48b)

Compared to Eq. (
12
), in Eq. (
48
) the Mathieu functions have been replaced by exponentials,

\displaystyle u_{\ell}\left(\frac{\varphi-\sigma(\mathbf{k})}{2},q(|\mathbf{k}|)\right)

\displaystyle\mapsto\mathchoice{\mathrm{e}^{\mathring{\imath}\ell\varphi}}{\operatorname{exp}\left(\mathring{\imath}\ell\varphi\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(\mathring{\imath}\ell\varphi\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(\mathring{\imath}\ell\varphi\right)}

(49a)

\displaystyle\widetilde{u}_{\ell}\left(\frac{\varphi-\sigma(\mathbf{k})}{2},q(|\mathbf{k}|)\right)

\displaystyle\mapsto\frac{\mathchoice{\mathrm{e}^{-\mathring{\imath}\ell\varphi}}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-\mathring{\imath}\ell\varphi\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-\mathring{\imath}\ell\varphi\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-\mathring{\imath}\ell\varphi\right)}}{2\pi}

(49b)

which are orthonormal irrespective of
\mathbf{k}
. The bare propagator of Eq. (
47
) can immediately be read off,

\left\langle\chi_{\ell}(\mathbf{k},\omega)\tilde{\chi}_{\ell_{0}}(\mathbf{k}_{0},\omega_{0})\right\rangle_{0}=\frac{\delta_{\ell,\ell_{0}}\delta\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22(\mathbf{k}+\mathbf{k}_{0})\delta\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22(\omega+\omega_{0})}{-\mathring{\imath}\omega+\mathsf{D}_{t}\mathbf{k}^{2}+\mathsf{D}_{r}\ell^{2}+r}=\delta_{\ell,\ell_{0}}\delta\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22(\mathbf{k}+\mathbf{k}_{0})\delta\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22(\omega+\omega_{0})H_{\ell}\left(\mathbf{k},\omega\right)\\
\triangleq\hbox to73.42pt{\vbox to31.87pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 34.46446pt\lower-15.71045pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {
{}{{}}{}
{}{}{}{}{{{}{}}}{}{}{{{}{}}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} {}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -31.5 0 L 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

\lxSVG@closescope }}}\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{

\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}

\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\ .

(50)

The perturbative part of the action Eq. (
10b
) includes the self-propulsion, but its precise form depends on whether the system is finite or infinite and thus left to be stated in the sections ahead.

II.2.1
Mean squared displacement

It is instructive to derive the MSD with harmonic action Eq. (
47
), orthogonal system Eq. (
48
) and perturbative action Eq. (
10b
), rewritten as

\mathcal{A}_{P2}=\int\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22^{2}k\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22^{2}k\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22^{2}k\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22^{2}k\,}\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22^{2}k^{\prime}\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22^{2}k^{\prime}\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22^{2}k^{\prime}\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22^{2}k^{\prime}\,}\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega\,}\sum_{\ell,\ell^{\prime}=-\infty}^{\infty}\chi_{\ell}(\mathbf{k},\omega)\tilde{\chi}_{\ell^{\prime}}(\mathbf{k}^{\prime},-\omega)\frac{w_{0}}{2}\delta\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22(\mathbf{k}+\mathbf{k}^{\prime})\big[-\mathring{\imath}k_{x}(\delta_{\ell^{\prime},\ell+1}+\delta_{\ell^{\prime},\ell-1})+k_{y}(\delta_{\ell^{\prime},\ell-1}-\delta_{\ell^{\prime},\ell+1})\big]

(51)

in the absence of an external potential. To ease notation, we may introduce the tumbling vertex

\displaystyle W_{\ell^{\prime},\ell}(\mathbf{k})

\displaystyle=-w_{0}\int\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}\varphi\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\varphi\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\varphi\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\varphi\,}\frac{\mathchoice{\mathrm{e}^{-\mathring{\imath}\ell^{\prime}\varphi}}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-\mathring{\imath}\ell^{\prime}\varphi\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-\mathring{\imath}\ell^{\prime}\varphi\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-\mathring{\imath}\ell^{\prime}\varphi\right)}}{2\pi}(\mathring{\imath}k_{x}\cos\varphi+\mathring{\imath}k_{y}\sin\varphi)\mathchoice{\mathrm{e}^{\mathring{\imath}\ell\varphi}}{\operatorname{exp}\left(\mathring{\imath}\ell\varphi\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(\mathring{\imath}\ell\varphi\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(\mathring{\imath}\ell\varphi\right)}

(52a)

\displaystyle=\frac{w_{0}}{2}\big[-\mathring{\imath}k_{x}(\delta_{\ell^{\prime},\ell+1}+\delta_{\ell^{\prime},\ell-1})+k_{y}(\delta_{\ell^{\prime},\ell-1}-\delta_{\ell^{\prime},\ell+1})\big]

(52b)

with
\mathbf{k}=(k_{x},k_{y})^{\mathsf{T}}
and
\mathbf{r}=(x,y)^{\mathsf{T}}
.
The expression
\mathring{\imath}k_{x}\cos\varphi+\mathring{\imath}k_{y}\sin\varphi
is due to
\mathbf{w}\cdot\nabla_{\mathbf{r}}
of the self-propulsion hitting
\mathchoice{\mathrm{e}^{\mathring{\imath}\mathbf{k}\mathbf{r}}}{\operatorname{exp}\left(\mathring{\imath}\mathbf{k}\mathbf{r}\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(\mathring{\imath}\mathbf{k}\mathbf{r}\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(\mathring{\imath}\mathbf{k}\mathbf{r}\right)}
, Eq. (
2
).
For the diagrammatics
we introduce the self-propulsion vertex

{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-8.5359pt}{-3.93301pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{66}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}
{}{}{}{}{{{}{}}}{}{}{{{}{}}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} {}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 11.81 0 L 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-4.3194pt}{5.87746pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{66}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{4.2095pt}{-10.87744pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{66}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}+\dots{\\
}\triangleq\delta\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22(\mathbf{k}+\mathbf{k}_{0})\delta\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22(\omega+\omega_{0})\Big(H_{\ell}\left(\mathbf{k},\omega\right)\delta_{\ell,\ell_{0}}+H_{\ell}\left(\mathbf{k},\omega\right)W_{\ell,\ell_{0}}(\mathbf{k})H_{\ell_{0}}\left(\mathbf{k},\omega\right)\\
+\sum_{\ell_{1}}H_{\ell}\left(\mathbf{k},\omega\right)W_{\ell,\ell_{1}}(\mathbf{k})H_{\ell_{1}}\left(\mathbf{k},\omega\right)W_{\ell_{1},\ell_{0}}(\mathbf{k})H_{\ell_{0}}\left(\mathbf{k},\omega\right)+\ldots\Big)

(54)

using the notation of Eq. (
50
).
A marginalisation over
\varphi
amounts to evaluating Eq. (
54
) at
\ell=0
, Eq. (
48
).
The MSD can be determined by double differentiation with respect to
\mathbf{k}
as done in Eqs. (
21
) and (
26
). This calculation is simplified by calculating the mean squared displacement in
x
only,
i.e.
taking
\partial_{k_{x}}^{2}
rather than
\partial_{k_{x}}^{2}+\partial_{k_{y}}^{2}
. This “trick”, however also requires uniform initialisation in
\varphi_{0}
to avoid any bias. In summary

\overline{\mathbf{r}^{2}}(t-t_{0})=2\overline{x^{2}}(t-t_{0})=-2\left.\partial_{k_{x}}^{2}\right|_{\begin{subarray}{c}k_{x}=0\\
k_{y}=0\end{subarray}}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}\varphi\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\varphi\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\varphi\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\varphi\,}\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}\varphi_{0}\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\varphi_{0}\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\varphi_{0}\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\varphi_{0}\,}\int\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22^{2}k_{0}\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22^{2}k_{0}\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22^{2}k_{0}\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22^{2}k_{0}\,}\left\langle\chi(\mathbf{k},\varphi,t)\tilde{\chi}(\mathbf{k}_{0},\varphi_{0},t_{0})\right\rangle

(55)

where the integral over
\mathbf{k}_{0}
is the inverse Fourier transform with
\mathbf{x}_{0}=\mathbf{0}
and is trivially taken by using
\delta\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22(\mathbf{k}+\mathbf{k}_{0})
due to translational invariance.

The two integrals over
\varphi
and
\varphi_{0}
in Eq. (
55
) amount to setting
\ell=0
and
\ell_{0}=0
, with all factors of
2\pi
cancelling. Of the three terms in Eq. (
54
), the second one, which is the first order correction in the self-propulsion carrying a single factor of the tumbling vertex
W_{\ell,\ell_{0}}(\mathbf{k})
, Eq. (
52a
), is odd in
k_{x}
. Differentiating twice with respect to
k_{x}
and evaluating at
k_{x}=k_{y}=0
will thus make it vanish. The third term is quadratic in
k_{x}
in the numerator and not singular in
k_{x}
in the denominator. The only way for the third term not to vanish at
k_{x}=k_{y}=0
is when
W_{\ell,\ell_{1}}(\mathbf{k})W_{\ell_{1},\ell_{0}}(\mathbf{k})
is differentiated twice with respect to
k_{x}
,

\left.\partial_{k_{x}}^{2}\right|_{\begin{subarray}{c}k_{x}=0\\
k_{y}=0\end{subarray}}W_{0,\ell_{1}}(\mathbf{k})W_{\ell_{1},0}(\mathbf{k})=\frac{-w_{0}^{2}}{2}\big[\delta_{1,\ell_{1}}+\delta_{-1,\ell_{1}}\big]\ .

(56)

No higher order terms in
w_{0}
can contribute, as they all carry higher powers of
k_{x}
as a pre-factor. We thus arrive at

\overline{\mathbf{r}^{2}}(t-t_{0})=\int\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega\,}\mathchoice{\mathrm{e}^{-\mathring{\imath}\omega(t-t_{0})}}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-\mathring{\imath}\omega(t-t_{0})\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-\mathring{\imath}\omega(t-t_{0})\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-\mathring{\imath}\omega(t-t_{0})\right)}\Big\{\frac{4\mathsf{D}_{t}}{(-\mathring{\imath}\omega+r)^{2}}+\frac{w_{0}^{2}}{(-\mathring{\imath}\omega+r)^{2}}\frac{2}{-\mathring{\imath}\omega+\mathsf{D}_{r}+r}\Big\}

(57)

where the term
2/(-\mathring{\imath}\omega+\mathsf{D}_{r}+r)
originates from

H_{\ell_{1}}\left(\mathbf{k},\omega\right)
at
\ell_{1}=-1,1
according to Eq. (
56
). Taking the inverse Fourier transform from
\omega
to
t-t_{0}
then indeed reproduces Eq. (
26
) in the limit
r\downarrow 0
with the repeated poles producing the desired factors of
t-t_{0}
. This confirms that even when the self-propulsion is dealt with perturbatively, observables can be derived in closed form. This is not a triviality, as
w_{0}^{2}t/\mathsf{D}_{t}
is a dimensionless quantity and thus might enter to all orders.
We proceed in the present framework by allowing for an external potential.

II.2.2
External Potential

In this section, we aim to re-derive the density
\rho_{0}(\mathbf{r})
of active Brownian Particles in an external potential, as initially determined in Section
II.1.2
. As the self-propulsion is a perturbation, the full perturbative action Eqs. (
10b
), (
51
) reads

\mathcal{A}_{P2}=\int\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega\,}\frac{1}{L^{4}}\sum_{\mathbf{n}_{1},\mathbf{n}_{2}}\sum_{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}=-\infty}^{\infty}\chi_{\ell_{1}}(\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}_{1}},\omega)\tilde{\chi}_{\ell_{2}}(\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}_{2}},-\omega)L^{2}\delta_{\mathbf{n}_{1}+\mathbf{n}_{2},\mathbf{0}}W_{\ell_{2},\ell_{1}}(\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}_{1}})\\
+\frac{1}{L^{6}}\sum_{\mathbf{n}_{1},\mathbf{n}_{2},\mathbf{n}_{3}}L^{2}\delta_{\mathbf{n}_{1}+\mathbf{n}_{2}+\mathbf{n}_{3},\mathbf{0}}\sum_{\ell_{1},\ell_{3}}(\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}_{1}}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}_{2}})\tilde{\chi}_{\ell_{1}}(\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}_{1}},-\omega)\Upsilon_{\mathbf{n}_{2}}\chi_{\ell_{3}}(\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}_{3}},\omega)

(58)

using the notation
\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}_{1}}=(k_{n_{1x}},k_{n_{1y}})^{\mathsf{T}}
.
The action Eq. (
58
) is based on
discrete Fourier modes similar to Eq. (
28
)
as the system’s volume is finite. It deviates from
Eq. (
28
)
but using Fourier modes for the director as in Eq. (
48
)

\displaystyle\chi(\mathbf{r},\varphi,t)

\displaystyle=\int\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega\,}\frac{1}{L^{2}}\sum_{\mathbf{n}\in\mathds{Z}^{2}}\sum_{\ell=-\infty}^{\infty}\mathchoice{\mathrm{e}^{-\mathring{\imath}\omega t}}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-\mathring{\imath}\omega t\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-\mathring{\imath}\omega t\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-\mathring{\imath}\omega t\right)}\mathchoice{\mathrm{e}^{\mathring{\imath}\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}}\cdot\mathbf{r}}}{\operatorname{exp}\left(\mathring{\imath}\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}}\cdot\mathbf{r}\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(\mathring{\imath}\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}}\cdot\mathbf{r}\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(\mathring{\imath}\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}}\cdot\mathbf{r}\right)}\mathchoice{\mathrm{e}^{\mathring{\imath}\ell\varphi}}{\operatorname{exp}\left(\mathring{\imath}\ell\varphi\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(\mathring{\imath}\ell\varphi\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(\mathring{\imath}\ell\varphi\right)}\chi_{\ell}(\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}},\omega)

(59a)

\displaystyle\tilde{\chi}(\mathbf{r},\varphi,t)

\displaystyle=\int\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega\,}\frac{1}{L^{2}}\sum_{\mathbf{n}\in\mathds{Z}^{2}}\sum_{\ell=-\infty}^{\infty}\mathchoice{\mathrm{e}^{-\mathring{\imath}\omega t}}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-\mathring{\imath}\omega t\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-\mathring{\imath}\omega t\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-\mathring{\imath}\omega t\right)}\mathchoice{\mathrm{e}^{\mathring{\imath}\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}}\cdot\mathbf{r}}}{\operatorname{exp}\left(\mathring{\imath}\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}}\cdot\mathbf{r}\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(\mathring{\imath}\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}}\cdot\mathbf{r}\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(\mathring{\imath}\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}}\cdot\mathbf{r}\right)}\frac{\mathchoice{\mathrm{e}^{-\mathring{\imath}\ell\varphi}}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-\mathring{\imath}\ell\varphi\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-\mathring{\imath}\ell\varphi\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-\mathring{\imath}\ell\varphi\right)}}{2\pi}\tilde{\chi}_{\ell}(\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}},\omega)\ .

(59b)

Compared to Eq. (
29b
), Eq. (
58
) has the great benefit of the diagonality of the angular modes in the potential term.

To calculate the particle density
\rho_{0}(\mathbf{r})
in the stationary state, Eq. (
34
), we expand the full propagator diagrammatically,

\displaystyle\left\langle\chi(\mathbf{k},\varphi,\omega)\tilde{\chi}(\mathbf{k}_{0},\varphi_{0},\omega_{0})\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\hbox to73.42pt{\vbox to31.87pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 34.46446pt\lower-15.71045pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {

(60e)

As in Section
II.1.2
, taking the limit
t_{0}\to-\infty
amounts to an amputation upon which any diagram vanishes that carries a dashed propagator before (
i.e.
to the right of) a source of momentum. As a result, only the five leftmost diagrams in Eq. (
60
) contribute at stationarity. In the following, we will restrict ourselves to diagrams with at most one bauble,
i.e.
to first order in the potential. Further, we will incorporate the self-propulsion to second order, leaving us with the four leftmost diagrams in Eqs. (
60a
)-(
60d
).

As seen in Section
II.1.2
, Eq. (
60a
) contributes to the density merely with
1/L^{2}
. The other three diagrams require more detailed attention.
Firstly, Eq. (
60b
) gives

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\triangleq\delta_{\ell,\ell_{0}}\delta\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22(\omega+\omega_{0})H_{\ell}\left(\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}},\omega\right)(-\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{p}})\Upsilon_{\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{p}}H_{\ell_{0}}\left(-\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{p}},\omega\right)\ ,

(61)

using Eq. (
50
) for
H_{\ell}\left(\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}},\omega\right)
. Upon taking the inverse Fourier transform in
\omega
and
\omega_{0}
, the limit
t_{0}\to-\infty
can be taken, replacing the incoming leg

H_{\ell_{0}}\left(\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{p}},\omega_{0}\right)
effectively by
\delta_{\mathbf{p},\mathbf{0}}\delta_{\ell_{0},0}
. After transforming from
\ell,\ell_{0}
to
\varphi,\varphi_{0}
and taking the integral over
\varphi
all factors of
2\pi
have cancelled, leaving of Eq. (
61
)

\lim_{t_{0}\to-\infty}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}\varphi\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\varphi\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\varphi\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\varphi\,}D_{b}(\mathbf{r},\varphi,t;\mathbf{r}_{0},\varphi_{0},t_{0})=\frac{1}{L^{4}}\sum_{\mathbf{n}}\mathchoice{\mathrm{e}^{\mathring{\imath}\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}}\cdot\mathbf{r}}}{\operatorname{exp}\left(\mathring{\imath}\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}}\cdot\mathbf{r}\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(\mathring{\imath}\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}}\cdot\mathbf{r}\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(\mathring{\imath}\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}}\cdot\mathbf{r}\right)}H_{0}\left(\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}},0\right)(-\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}})\Upsilon_{\mathbf{n}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r\downarrow 0}}{{=}}-\frac{1}{L^{4}}\sum_{\mathbf{n}\neq\mathbf{0}}\mathchoice{\mathrm{e}^{\mathring{\imath}\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}}\cdot\mathbf{r}}}{\operatorname{exp}\left(\mathring{\imath}\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}}\cdot\mathbf{r}\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(\mathring{\imath}\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}}\cdot\mathbf{r}\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(\mathring{\imath}\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}}\cdot\mathbf{r}\right)}\frac{\Upsilon_{\mathbf{n}}}{\mathsf{D}_{t}}

(62)

as
H_{0}\left(\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}},0\right)=(\mathsf{D}_{t}k_{\mathbf{n}}^{2}+r)^{-1}
, whose factor
k_{\mathbf{n}}^{-2}
cancels with

\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}}
unless
\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}}=\mathbf{0}
, in which case the expression vanishes.
Eq. (
62
) is the lowest order correction in both
\Upsilon
and
w_{0}
to the density.

) and (

), only the latter contributes once

forces

and
\int\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}\varphi\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\varphi\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\varphi\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\varphi\,}
forces
\ell=0
,
because the single tumbling vertex, Eq. (
52a
), requires the angular modes to be offset by
1
. The only diagram left to consider is thus
