\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\!\!\!\ ,

(96)

differing from Eq. (
50
) only by the additional factor
\delta_{m,m_{0}}
and by the eigenvalue of the spherical Laplacian being
\ell(\ell+1)
rather than
\ell^{2}
.
In the absence of an external potential the perturbative part of the action reads

\mathcal{A}_{P2}=\int\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22^{3}k\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22^{3}k\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22^{3}k\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22^{3}k\,}\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}^{3}k^{\prime}\,}{\!\mathrm{d}^{3}k^{\prime}\,}{\!\mathrm{d}^{3}k^{\prime}\,}{\!\mathrm{d}^{3}k^{\prime}\,}\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega\,}\sum_{\ell,\ell^{\prime}=0}^{\infty}\sum_{m=-\ell}^{\ell}\sum_{m^{\prime}=-\ell^{\prime}}^{\ell^{\prime}}\chi_{\ell}^{m}(\mathbf{k},\omega)\tilde{\chi}_{\ell^{\prime}}^{m^{\prime}}(\mathbf{k}^{\prime},-\omega)\delta\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22(\mathbf{k}+\mathbf{k}^{\prime})W_{\ell^{\prime},\ell}^{m^{\prime},m}(\mathbf{k})

(97)

similar to Eq. (
51
)
with tumbling vertex

W_{\ell^{\prime},\ell}^{m^{\prime},m}(\mathbf{k})=-w_{0}\int_{0}^{\pi}\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}\theta\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\theta\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\theta\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\theta\,}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}\varphi\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\varphi\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\varphi\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\varphi\,}\sin\!\theta\Big(Y_{\ell^{\prime}}^{m^{\prime}}(\theta,\varphi)\Big)^{*}(\mathring{\imath}k_{x}\sin\!\theta\cos\!\varphi+\mathring{\imath}k_{y}\sin\!\theta\sin\varphi+\mathring{\imath}k_{z}\cos\!\theta)Y_{\ell}^{m}(\theta,\varphi)

(98)

In principle, any product like
\sin\theta\cos\varphi Y_{\ell}^{m}(\theta,\varphi)
can be written in terms of a sum of spherical harmonics (see App.
B
),

\displaystyle\sin\!\theta\cos\!\varphi Y_{\ell}^{m}(\theta,\varphi)

\displaystyle=\sum_{L,M}\sqrt{\frac{2\ell+1}{2(2L+1)}}c_{1,0,\ell,0}^{L,0}Y_{L}^{M}(\theta,\varphi)\bigg(-c_{1,1,\ell,m}^{L,M}+c_{1,-1,\ell,m}^{L,M}\bigg)\ ,

(99a)

\displaystyle\sin\!\theta\sin\!\varphi Y_{\ell}^{m}(\theta,\varphi)

\displaystyle=\mathring{\imath}\sum_{L,M}\sqrt{\frac{2\ell+1}{2(2L+1)}}c_{1,0,\ell,0}^{L,0}Y_{L}^{M}(\theta,\varphi)\bigg(c_{1,1,\ell,m}^{L,M}+c_{1,-1,\ell,m}^{L,M}\bigg)\ ,

(99b)

\displaystyle\cos\!\theta Y_{\ell}^{m}(\theta,\varphi)

\displaystyle=\sum_{L,M}\sqrt{\frac{2\ell+1}{2L+1}}c_{1,0,\ell,0}^{L,0}c_{1,0,\ell,m}^{L,M}Y_{L}^{M}(\theta,\varphi)\ ,

(99c)

where
c_{\ell^{\prime},m^{\prime},\ell,m}^{L,M}
are the Clebsch-Gordan coefficients, further detailed
Racah:1942
in App.
B
.

The coupling matrix
W_{\ell^{\prime},\ell}^{m^{\prime},m}(\mathbf{k})
can therefore be written as

W_{\ell^{\prime},\ell}^{m^{\prime},m}(\mathbf{k})=W_{\ell^{\prime},\ell}^{m^{\prime},m}(k_{x}\mathbf{e}_{x})+W_{\ell^{\prime},\ell}^{m^{\prime},m}(k_{y}\mathbf{e}_{y})+W_{\ell^{\prime},\ell}^{m^{\prime},m}(k_{z}\mathbf{e}_{z})\ .

(100)

the simplest among the three terms is

\displaystyle W_{\ell^{\prime},\ell}^{m^{\prime},m}(k_{z}\mathbf{e}_{z})

\displaystyle=-\mathring{\imath}w_{0}k_{z}\sum_{L,M}\sqrt{\frac{2\ell+1}{2L+1}}c_{1,0,\ell,0}^{L,0}c_{1,0,\ell,m}^{L,M}\delta_{m^{\prime},M}\delta_{\ell^{\prime},L}

(101a)

\displaystyle=-\mathring{\imath}w_{0}k_{z}\delta_{m,m^{\prime}}\left\{\sqrt{\frac{(\ell+1+m)(\ell+1-m)}{(2\ell+1)(2\ell+3)}}\delta_{\ell^{\prime},\ell+1}+\sqrt{\frac{(\ell+m)(\ell-m)}{(2\ell+1)(2\ell-1)}}\delta_{\ell^{\prime},\ell-1}\right\}\ ,

(101b)

with the other two stated in App.
B
.
With this framework in place, we proceed to calculate the MSD and the density at stationarity.

III.2.1
Mean squared displacement

We follow Section
II.2.1
to calculate the MSD of active Brownian particles in three dimensions, which is

\overline{\mathbf{r}^{2}}(t-t_{0})=3\overline{z^{2}}(t-t_{0})=-3\left.\partial_{k_{z}}^{2}\right|_{\mathbf{k}=0}\int_{0}^{\pi}\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}\theta\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\theta\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\theta\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\theta\,}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}\varphi\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\varphi\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\varphi\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\varphi\,}\sin\theta\,\frac{1}{4\pi}\int_{0}^{\pi}\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}\theta_{0}\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\theta_{0}\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\theta_{0}\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\theta_{0}\,}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}\varphi_{0}\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\varphi_{0}\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\varphi_{0}\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\varphi_{0}\,}\sin\theta_{0}\,\int\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22^{3}k_{0}\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22^{3}k_{0}\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22^{3}k_{0}\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22^{3}k_{0}\,}\left\langle\chi(\mathbf{k},\theta,\varphi,t)\tilde{\chi}(\mathbf{k}_{0},\theta_{0},\varphi_{0},t_{0})\right\rangle

(102)

corresponding to Eq. (
55
).
With the help of the diagrammatics Eq. (
54
) and the reasoning before Eq. (
56
), this can essentially be done by inspection. The derivative of the bare propagator will contribute
-3(-2\mathsf{D}_{t}t)
to the MSD. The only non-trivial contribution is due to

, which has a very simple structure because the integration over
\theta
and
\varphi
, forces
\ell=0
and
m=0
, given that
Y_{\ell}^{m}
are orthogonal and
Y_{0}^{0}=1/\sqrt{4\pi}
and so integrals over
Y_{\ell}^{m}
vanish unless
\ell=m=0
. The same reasoning applies to
\ell_{0}=m_{0}=0
.
The summation that appears in the last term of Eq. (
54
) therefore becomes

\sum_{\ell_{1}=0}^{\infty}\sum_{m_{1}=-\ell_{1}}^{\ell_{1}}W_{0,\ell_{1}}^{0,m_{1}}(k_{z}\mathbf{e}_{z})H_{\ell_{1}}^{m_{1}}\left(k_{z}\mathbf{e}_{z},\omega\right)W_{\ell_{1},0}^{m_{1},0}(k_{z}\mathbf{e}_{z})=-w_{0}^{2}k_{z}^{2}\frac{1}{3}\frac{1}{-\mathring{\imath}\omega+\mathsf{D}_{t}k_{z}^{2}+2\mathsf{D}_{r}}

(103)

because only
\ell_{1}=1
contributes, resulting in a factor
\ell_{1}(\ell_{1}+1)=2
in front of
\mathsf{D}_{r}
in the propagator.
Differentiating twice with respect to
k_{z}
and taking the inverse Fourier transform of the expression with the bare propagators attached,

-\frac{2}{3}w_{0}^{2}\lim_{r\downarrow 0}\int\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega\,}\mathchoice{\mathrm{e}^{-\mathring{\imath}\omega t}}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-\mathring{\imath}\omega t\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-\mathring{\imath}\omega t\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-\mathring{\imath}\omega t\right)}\frac{1}{(-\mathring{\imath}\omega+r)^{2}}\frac{1}{-\mathring{\imath}\omega+2\mathsf{D}_{r}+r}=\frac{2}{3}w_{0}^{2}\left\{\frac{-t}{2\mathsf{D}_{r}}+\frac{1}{4\mathsf{D}_{r}^{2}}-\frac{1}{4\mathsf{D}_{r}^{2}}\mathchoice{\mathrm{e}^{-2\mathsf{D}_{r}t}}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-2\mathsf{D}_{r}t\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-2\mathsf{D}_{r}t\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-2\mathsf{D}_{r}t\right)}\right\}

(104)

finally reproduces Eq. (
79
) exactly.

III.2.2
External potential

Calculating the stationary density
\rho_{0}(\mathbf{r})
in the presence of an external potential perturbatively in the self-propulsion and the potential follows the pattern outlined in Section
II.2.2
. Because the volume is finite, the representation we choose for the fields is

\displaystyle\chi(\mathbf{r},\varphi,t)

\displaystyle=\int\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega\,}\frac{1}{L^{3}}\sum_{\mathbf{n}\in\mathds{Z}^{3}}\sum_{\ell=0}^{\infty}\sum_{m=-\ell}^{\ell}\mathchoice{\mathrm{e}^{-\mathring{\imath}\omega t}}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-\mathring{\imath}\omega t\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-\mathring{\imath}\omega t\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-\mathring{\imath}\omega t\right)}\mathchoice{\mathrm{e}^{\mathring{\imath}\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}}\cdot\mathbf{r}}}{\operatorname{exp}\left(\mathring{\imath}\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}}\cdot\mathbf{r}\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(\mathring{\imath}\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}}\cdot\mathbf{r}\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(\mathring{\imath}\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}}\cdot\mathbf{r}\right)}(4\pi)^{1/2}Y_{\ell}^{m}(\theta,\varphi)\chi_{\ell}^{m}(\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}},\omega)

(105a)

\displaystyle\tilde{\chi}(\mathbf{r},\varphi,t)

\displaystyle=\int\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega\,}\frac{1}{L^{3}}\sum_{\mathbf{n}\in\mathds{Z}^{3}}\sum_{\ell=0}^{\infty}\sum_{m=-\ell}^{\ell}\mathchoice{\mathrm{e}^{-\mathring{\imath}\omega t}}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-\mathring{\imath}\omega t\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-\mathring{\imath}\omega t\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-\mathring{\imath}\omega t\right)}\mathchoice{\mathrm{e}^{\mathring{\imath}\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}}\cdot\mathbf{r}}}{\operatorname{exp}\left(\mathring{\imath}\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}}\cdot\mathbf{r}\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(\mathring{\imath}\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}}\cdot\mathbf{r}\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(\mathring{\imath}\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}}\cdot\mathbf{r}\right)}(4\pi)^{-1/2}\Big(Y_{\ell}^{m}(\theta,\varphi)\Big)^{*}\tilde{\chi}_{\ell}^{m}(\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}},\omega)\ .

(105b)

rather than Eq. (
91
), with the sums over
\mathbf{n}\in\mathds{Z}^{3}
replacing the integrals over
\mathbf{k}
.

Given the orthogonality of the spherical harmonics, the perturbative part of the action now reads

\mathcal{A}_{P2}=\int\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega\,}\frac{1}{L^{6}}\sum_{\mathbf{n}_{1},\mathbf{n}_{2}}\sum_{\ell,\ell^{\prime}=0}^{\infty}\sum_{m=-\ell}^{\ell}\sum_{m^{\prime}=-\ell^{\prime}}^{\ell^{\prime}}\chi_{\ell}^{m}(\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}_{1}},\omega)\tilde{\chi}_{\ell^{\prime}}^{m^{\prime}}(\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}_{2}},-\omega)\delta_{\mathbf{n}_{1}+\mathbf{n}_{2},\mathbf{0}}W_{\ell^{\prime},\ell}^{m^{\prime},m}(\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}_{1}})\\
+\frac{1}{L^{9}}\sum_{\mathbf{n}_{1},\mathbf{n}_{2},\mathbf{n}_{3}}L^{3}\delta_{\mathbf{n}_{1}+\mathbf{n}_{2}+\mathbf{n}_{3},\mathbf{0}}\sum_{\ell_{1},\ell_{3}}\sum_{m=-\ell_{3}}^{\ell_{3}}\sum_{m^{\prime}=-\ell_{1}}^{\ell_{1}}(\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}_{1}}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}_{2}})\tilde{\chi}^{m^{\prime}}_{\ell_{1}}(\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}_{1}},-\omega)\Upsilon_{\mathbf{n}_{2}}\chi^{m}_{\ell_{3}}(\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}_{3}},\omega)

(106)

corresponding to Eq. (
58
) in two dimensions, but with the tumbling vertex
W_{\ell^{\prime},\ell}^{m^{\prime},m}(\mathbf{k})
given by Eq. (
98
).

In principle,
\Upsilon_{\mathbf{n}}
is arbitrary, but in order to stay with the simple form of
W_{\ell,\ell^{\prime}}^{m,m^{\prime}}(k_{z}\mathbf{e}_{z})
, Eq. (
101b
), we restrict
\Upsilon(\mathbf{r})
to vary only with
z
, making again use of the notation Eq. (
85
). The sole contribution that needs our attention is due to the single term Eq. (
63
), which draws on Eq. (
103
).

Using
W_{0,1}^{0,0}(k_{z}\mathbf{e}_{z})W_{1,0}^{0,0}(k_{z}\mathbf{e}_{z})=-w_{0}^{2}k_{z}^{2}/3
, the density can be read off Eq. (
66b
)

\rho_{0}(\mathbf{r})=L^{-3}-\frac{1}{L^{4}}\sum_{n_{z}\neq 0}\mathchoice{\mathrm{e}^{\mathring{\imath}k_{n_{z}}z}}{\operatorname{exp}\left(\mathring{\imath}k_{n_{z}}z\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(\mathring{\imath}k_{n_{z}}z\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(\mathring{\imath}k_{n_{z}}z\right)}\frac{\Upsilon_{n_{z}}}{\mathsf{D}_{t}}\left\{1-\frac{w_{0}^{2}}{3\mathsf{D}_{t}}(\mathsf{D}_{t}k_{n_{z}}^{2}+2\mathsf{D}_{r})^{-1}\right\}+\mathcal{O}\!\left(w_{0}^{4}\right)+\ldots

(107)

with the denominator of the fraction
w_{0}^{2}/(3\mathsf{D}_{t})
changed from
w_{0}^{2}/(2\mathsf{D}_{t})
in Eq. (
66b
), and the internal propagator changed to

(\mathsf{D}_{t}k_{n_{z}}^{2}+2\mathsf{D}_{r})^{-1}
from

(\mathsf{D}_{t}k_{\mathbf{n}}^{2}+\mathsf{D}_{r})^{-1}
.
Comparing to
Eq. (
162
), Eq. (
107
)
reveals corrections to order
w_{0}^{2}
beyond replacing
\mathsf{D}_{t}
at
\mathbf{w}_{0}=\mathbf{0}
in Eq. (
162
) by
\mathsf{D}^{\text{3D}}_{\text{eff}}
Eq. (
80
).

Eq. (
107
) correctly reproduces the result based on the expansion in ”three-dimensional Mathieu functions”, Eq. (
89
), as

1-\frac{w_{0}^{2}}{3\mathsf{D}_{t}}(\mathsf{D}_{t}k_{n_{z}}^{2}+2\mathsf{D}_{r})^{-1}+\mathcal{O}\!\left(w_{0}^{4}\right)=\mathsf{D}_{t}\Bigg\{\big(\mathsf{D}_{t}+w_{0}^{2}/(6\mathsf{D}_{r})+\mathcal{O}\!\left(q^{4}\right)/k_{n_{z}}^{2}\big)^{-1}+\frac{k_{n_{z}}^{4}w_{0}^{2}}{12\mathsf{D}_{r}^{2}}\Big[\big(\mathsf{D}_{t}k_{n_{z}}^{2}+w_{0}^{2}k_{n_{z}}^{2}/(6\mathsf{D}_{r})+\mathcal{O}\!\left(q^{4}\right)\big)^{-1}\\
-\big(\mathsf{D}_{t}k_{n_{z}}^{2}+2\mathsf{D}_{r}+\mathsf{D}_{r}\mathcal{O}\!\left(q^{2}\right)\big)^{-1}\Big]\Bigg\}\ .

(108)

IV
Discussion and Conclusion

There are two main perspectives on the present results, first about the concrete observables calculated and second about the formalism.

Observables:

In this work we calculated the mean squared displacement and the stationary density of ABPs in two and three dimensions using two distinct methods. The MSD calculations were performed primarily for methodological purposes, although the full time-dependence may be a new result.
Bechinger:2016
;
FrankeGruler:1990
;
Cates:2013
;
ZhangPruessner:2022
.

In two dimensions, we determined the exact MSD of ABPs using Mathieu functions with full time dependence in Eq. (
26
) as well as using the perturbative approach leading to Eq. (
57
). In three dimensions, similarly to the two dimensional case, we used ”three-dimensional Mathieu functions” for computation of the exact full time-dependent MSD in Eq. (
79
) and spherical harmonics in Eq. (
104
). The effective diffusion constants for both, the two and three dimensional case in Eq. (
27
) and Eq. (
80
) respectively, are identical to the Run-and-Tumble motion case using appropriate conversion
ZhangPruessner:2022
;
Cates:2013
.

Generalizing to
d>3
dimensions using the perturbative approach and so-called hyperspherical harmonics
Wen:1985
;
Askey:1983
, the expression for the MSD of ABPs is
Askey:1983

\overline{\mathbf{r}^{2}}(t)=2dDt+\frac{2w_{0}^{2}}{(d-1)^{2}D_{r}^{2}}\bigg(\mathchoice{\mathrm{e}^{-(d-1)D_{r}t}}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-(d-1)D_{r}t\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-(d-1)D_{r}t\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-(d-1)D_{r}t\right)}-1+(d-1)D_{r}t\bigg)\ .

(109)

with the details to be found in
ZZTHESIS
. The effective diffusion constant

\mathsf{D}^{\text{dD}}_{\text{eff}}=D+\frac{w_{0}^{2}}{d(d-1)D_{r}}\quad\text{for}\quad d\geq 2

(110)

is consistent with
Cates:2013
.

The MSD was calculated irrespective of the final orientation of the ABP and integrated about the initial angle without loss of generality. This leads to a significant reduction in the number of terms in the final MSD expression as discussed after Eq. (
21
) in two dimensions. The same method was used in the three dimensional case with “three-dimensional Mathieu functions” in Section
III.1.1
. For the self-propulsion perturbative case, it is a matter of convenience to determine the mean squared displacement in only one spatial direction, which however requires uniform initialisation in order to avoid bias, as discussed in two dimensions after Eq. (
59
). The same approach was taken in three dimensions in Section
III.2.1
.

The calculation of the stationary density in the presence of the external potential is, to our knowledge, a new result with the present generality. The stationary density in two dimensions was computed perturbatively in presence of small potentials in
Eqs. (
44
), (
46
) and (
66
) to first order in the potential. These expressions differ only in that they are expanded in different parameters, i.e. the parameter
q
of the Mathieu function in (
44
), the spatial momentum
\mathbf{k}_{n}
in (
46
) and
\mathbf{k},w_{0}
in (
66
).
We further assumed a finite volume
L^{d}
, to guarantee a stationary state in the absence of a potential, similar to drift-diffusive case discussed in Section
C
. As a result of using Fourier sums, the potentials and the resulting observables are periodic.
The two-dimensional MSD is in agreement with experimental results
Howse:2007
.

In three dimensions, the stationary density calculation with the “three dimensional Mathieu-functions” in the external potential was simplified by allowing the potential to vary in only one direction, Eq. (
85
), App.
A
. In two dimensions, this assumption was avoided by the re-orientation of the coordinate system in Section
II.1
.
In the perturbative approach, Section
III.2
, the above-mentioned assumption helped avoid having to compute general Clebsch-Gordan coefficients in Eq. (
98
), but determined nevertheless in App.
B
.

The stationary density in three dimensions in Eq. (
89
) is calculated to first order in the potential and expanded to second order in
\mathbf{q}
. The perturbative calculation in the self-propulsion results in Eq. (
107
). The restriction on the potential in three dimensions is more easily lifted in the perturbation theory using spherical harmonics, since the Clebsch-Gordan coefficients can be derived without much ado. On the other hand, characterising the ”three-dimensional Mathieu functions” requires at the very least a careful analysis of the completeness of the eigensystem, more easily done for
\mathbf{q}\parallel\mathbf{e}_{z}
.

Despite the restrictions on the potential, the perturbation theory covers a wide class of potentials. As is analysed in detail for drift-diffusive systems in Section
C
,
the resummed pertubation theory recovers known closed form expressions, including
a confining potential. The most interesting case of “active sedimentation” in a gravitational potential in a finite vessel can be realised within the restrictions mentioned above by long-stretched, periodic ratchet in the
z
-direction with period
L
,

\Upsilon(\mathbf{r})=\mu mg\mathbf{r}\cdot\mathbf{e}_{z}

(111)

with particle mass
m
, gravitational acceleration
g
and mobility
\mu
. With
\mathbf{r}
confined to
[0,L)^{d}
by periodicity,

\mathbf{r}\cdot\mathbf{e}_{z}=-\epsilon L
with
\epsilon\in[0,1)
is mapped to
\Upsilon((1-\epsilon)L\mathbf{e}_{z})
by periodicity in Eq. (
30
), so that the potential ”energy” Eq. (
111
) jumps to the maximum right after reaching the minimum for decreasing
t
, implementing a sharp potential wall at
z=0
, the bottom of the vessel.

The “barometric formulas” in Eqs. (
46
) and (
90
)
show the corrections to the density that are generically due to the activity. Those cannot possibly affect the
0
-mode, which is explicitly excluded from the summation.
To leading order the density of ABPs behaves like that of an equilibrium system with no self-propulsion,
\mathbf{w}_{0}=\mathbf{0}
and the translational diffusion constant
\mathsf{D}_{t}
, Eq. (
162
), replaced by the effective diffusion constant
\mathsf{D}^{\text{}}_{\text{eff}}
, Eq. (
27
) and (
80
).
Terms beyond that are generically due to the non-equilibrium nature of active matter.
The lowest order correction is quadratic in
k_{\mathbf{n}}
, namely
w_{0}^{2}k_{\mathbf{n}}^{2}/(2\mathsf{D}_{r}^{2})
in two dimensions and
w_{0}^{2}k_{\mathbf{n}}^{2}/(12\mathsf{D}_{r}^{2})
in three dimensions. It might be interesting to investigate how this can be measured in slowly varying potentials where contributions in large
\mathbf{n}
are suppressed because
\Upsilon_{\mathbf{n}}
vanishes there.

Methodology

We have introduced all ingredients to set up a field theory of ABPs, in particular: the action, the propagators, the projections of the special functions such as

\Delta_{\ell_{1},\ell_{3}}(-\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}_{1}},-\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}_{3}})
,
Eqs. (
31
) and (
83
)), and the tumble vertices (such as

W_{\ell^{\prime},\ell}(\mathbf{k})
,
Eqs. (
52a
) and (
98
). As well as providing the concrete mathematical framework, our derivations above highlight the advantages and disadvantages of using special functions rather than a perturbation theory. We will briefly summarise these here.

Using
special functions
,
i.e.
the Mathieu functions,
Section
II.1
and Section
III.1
,
has the benefit that the bare propagator already contains the self-propulsion. As long as space
\mathbf{r}
and its boundary conditions can be implemented through a Fourier-transform, this is the most efficient way to deal with
free
problems, wrapping the self-propulsion in the properties of certain special functions. The resulting bare propagator is the exact and complete characterisation of the free stochastic particle movement.
When the special functions are well-known, literature provides the relevant properties of functions and eigenvalues. When they are not well-known, they can be determined
perturbatively
in (hyper-)spherical harmonics, effectively performing the perturbation theory that would otherwise be done explicitly at the level of diagrams. In return, the wealth of literature on Sturm-Liouville problems is readily applicable, as touched on in Eq. (
119
).

We were able to avoid having to determine the “three-dimensional Mathieu functions” in greater detail by using a preferred direction, which generally requires integration of final and/or initial orientation. Thanks to the arbitrary function
\sigma(\mathbf{k})

in Eq. (
12
), this was avoided two dimensions, where we could use the well-known Mathieu functions. This may be related to the metric of a
d
-dimensional sphere to be curved only in
d>2
. In three dimensions, despite having
two
arbitrary functions
\sigma(\mathbf{k})
and
\tau(\mathbf{k})
in Eq. (
70
), an elegant simplification of the eigenproblem does not seem to be available.

Using a
perturbation theory
,
i.e.
expanding diagrams in
w_{0}
,
Section
II.2
and Section
III.2
,
means that even the free particle has to be treated explicitly perturbatively.
Although the full propagator now contains infinitely many terms, this does
necessarily translate to infinitely many terms for every observable, as seen, for example, in the fully time-dependent MSD calculated exactly using perturbation theory in two and three dimensions, Section
II.2.1
and Section
III.2.1
respectively. The big advantage of using a perturbation theory is that orthogonality is not spoiled by a lack of momentum conservation, as is caused by an external potential or interaction. Such a lack of momentum conservation is what necessitates the introduction of the projection
\Delta_{\ell_{1},\ell_{3}}(-\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}_{1}},-\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}_{3}})
in the case of special functions, Eq. (
32
)
and similarly (
82
),
but this does not happen when using a perturbation theory, Eqs. (
58
) and (
106
).

It is striking that the perturbation theory in weak potentials recovers the barometric formula for a binding potential (App.
C
).
The calculation of such stationary features is facilitated by the amputation mechanism as discussed in Section
II.1.2
and similarly used in
ZhangPruessner:2022
.

Outlook:

We are currently working on a field theory of
interacting
ABPs
displaying features of the Vicsek Model
Vicsek:1995
;
Ginelli:2016
. As the upper critical dimension there is
d_{c}=4
, we aim to generalise the spherical harmonics to higher dimensions. This would also allow the use of dimensional regularisation in a renormalised field theory. While some characteristics of such hyperspherical functions follow a systematic pattern, allowing the spatial dimension to vary continuously like
d=4-\epsilon
represents a significant challenge.

Appendix A
Three-dimensional Mathieu functions

In the following, we want to characterise the ”three-dimensional Mathieu functions”, which we call that only because the eigenfunctions of the equations below in two dimensions are the well-known Mathieu-functions
[
11
,
12
]
.

We will first state the defining equations and then set out to calculate the eigenfunctions. Firstly, we demand that
u_{\ell}^{m}\left(\theta,\varphi,\mathbf{q}\right)
are eigenfunctions,
Eq. (
74
)

\Big(-\mathbf{q}\cdot\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\sin\!\theta\cos\!\varphi\\
\sin\!\theta\sin\!\varphi\\
\cos\!\theta\end{array}\right)+\frac{1}{\sin\!\theta}\partial_{\theta}\sin\!\theta\ \partial_{\theta}+\frac{1}{\sin^{2}\!\theta}\partial_{\varphi}^{2}\Big)u_{\ell}^{m}\left(\theta,\varphi,\mathbf{q}\right)=-\lambda_{\ell}^{m}(\mathbf{q})u_{\ell}^{m}\left(\theta,\varphi,\mathbf{q}\right)\ .

(112)

Further, we demand normalisation,

\int_{0}^{\pi}\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}\theta\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\theta\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\theta\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\theta\,}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}\varphi\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\varphi\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\varphi\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\varphi\,}\sin\!\theta\ u_{\ell}^{m}\left(\theta,\varphi,\mathbf{q}\right)\widetilde{u}_{\ell^{\prime}}^{m^{\prime}}\left(\theta,\varphi,\mathbf{q}\right)=\delta_{\ell,\ell^{\prime}}\delta_{m,m^{\prime}}

(113)

with Eq. (
71
) to maintain close correspondence to the two-dimensional case Eq. (
24
),

\widetilde{u}_{\ell}^{m}\left(\theta,\varphi,\mathbf{q}(\mathbf{k})\right)=\frac{1}{4\pi}u_{\ell}^{m}\left(\theta,\varphi,\mathbf{q}(\mathbf{k})\right)

(114)

and
orthogonality following from the self-adjointness of the differential operator Eq. (
112
) under the scalar product Eq. (
113
). With that, all of Sturm-Liouville theory
[
21
]
is at our disposal.
We will index these eigenfunctions with
\ell=0,1,\ldots
and
m\in\mathds{Z}
.

For
\mathbf{q}=\mathbf{0}
vanishing, Eq. (
112
) is the eigen-equation of the spherical harmonics
[
12
]
. We therefore choose to express the three-dimensional Mathieu functions in spherical harmonics
Y_{j}^{k}(\theta,\varphi)
,

u_{\ell}^{m}\left(\theta,\varphi,\mathbf{q}\right)=\sqrt{4\pi}\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\sum_{k=-j}^{j}A_{\ell,j}^{m,k}(\mathbf{q})Y_{j}^{k}(\theta,\varphi)=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\sum_{k=-j}^{j}A_{\ell,j}^{m,k}(\mathbf{q})\sqrt{\frac{(2j+1)(j-k)!}{(j+k)!}}P_{j}^{k}(\cos\!\theta)\mathchoice{\mathrm{e}^{\mathring{\imath}k\varphi}}{\operatorname{exp}\left(\mathring{\imath}k\varphi\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(\mathring{\imath}k\varphi\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(\mathring{\imath}k\varphi\right)}

(115)

with the factor of
\sqrt{4\pi}
solely to ease notation and

P_{\ell}^{m}(z)
denoting the associated Legendre polynomials.
For
\mathbf{q}=\mathbf{0}
,

A_{\ell,j}^{m,k}(\mathbf{q})
is diagonal, so that

\displaystyle A_{\ell,j}^{m,k}(\mathbf{q})

\displaystyle=\left(\delta_{\ell,j}\delta_{m,k}+\mathcal{O}\!\left(\mathbf{q}\right)\right)

(116a)

\displaystyle\lambda_{\ell}^{m}(\mathbf{q})

\displaystyle=\ell(\ell+1)+\mathcal{O}\!\left(\mathbf{q}\right)\ ,

(116b)

where we assume that the coefficients
A_{\ell,j}^{m,k}(\mathbf{q})
and eigenvalues
\lambda_{\ell}^{m}(\mathbf{q})
can be expanded in small
\mathbf{q}
. Explicitly, for
\mathbf{q}=\mathbf{0}
,

\displaystyle u_{\ell}^{m}\left(\theta,\varphi,\mathbf{0}\right)

\displaystyle=\sqrt{4\pi}Y_{\ell}^{m}(\theta,\varphi)

(117a)

\displaystyle\widetilde{u}_{\ell}^{m}\left(\theta,\varphi,\mathbf{0}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{4\pi}}\big(Y_{\ell}^{m}(\theta,\varphi)\big)^{*}\ ,

(117b)

with the asterisk denoting the complex conjugate and indeed

\int_{0}^{\pi}\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}\theta\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\theta\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\theta\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\theta\,}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}\varphi\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\varphi\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\varphi\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\varphi\,}\sin\!\theta u_{\ell}^{m}\left(\theta,\varphi,\mathbf{0}\right)\widetilde{u}_{\ell^{\prime}}^{m^{\prime}}\left(\theta,\varphi,\mathbf{0}\right)=\delta_{\ell,\ell^{\prime}}\delta_{m,m^{\prime}}\ .

(118)

We focus henceforth on those
\mathbf{q}
that have no projection in the
x
and
y
directions,
\mathbf{q}=(0,0,q)^{\mathsf{T}}=q\mathbf{e}_{z}
, which simplifies Eq. (
112
) dramatically, rendering it similar to the expression in two dimensions, Eq. (
17
).
Only the
\theta
-dependence is affected by the presence of
\mathbf{q}\parallel\mathbf{e}_{z}
, so that

A_{\ell,j}^{m,k}(\mathbf{q})\propto\delta_{m,k}
is diagonal in
m,k
for such
\mathbf{q}
,
resulting in a simple structure and indexing of the azimuthal dependence,
i.e.
on
\varphi
,

u_{\ell}^{m}\left(\theta,\varphi,q\mathbf{e}_{z}\right)=\mathchoice{\mathrm{e}^{\mathring{\imath}m\varphi}}{\operatorname{exp}\left(\mathring{\imath}m\varphi\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(\mathring{\imath}m\varphi\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(\mathring{\imath}m\varphi\right)}\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}A_{\ell,j}^{m,m}(q\mathbf{e}_{z})\sqrt{\frac{(2j+1)(j-m)!}{(j+m)!}}P_{j}^{m}(\cos\!\theta)\ .

(119)

Because we will integrate
u_{\ell}^{m}\left(\theta,\varphi,q\mathbf{e}_{z}\right)
over
\varphi
, we can further narrow down the scope of this section to
m=0
, which simplifies the square root in Eq. (
119
) to
\sqrt{2j+1}
.

Because the Legendre polynomials obey

cos\theta P_{\ell}^{m}=\frac{\ell-m+1}{2\ell+1}P_{\ell+1}^{m}+\frac{\ell+m}{2\ell+1}P_{\ell-1}^{m}\ ,

(120)

the eigen-equation Eq. (
112
) for
m=0
and
\mathbf{q}=q\mathbf{e}_{z}
with Eq. (
115
)
is simply

0=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}A_{\ell,j}^{0,0}(q\mathbf{e}_{z})\sqrt{2j+1}\Bigg(q\frac{j+1}{2j+1}P_{j+1}^{0}(\cos\!\theta)+q\frac{j}{2j+1}P_{j-1}^{0}(\cos\!\theta)+\big(j(j+1)-\lambda_{\ell}^{0}(q\mathbf{e}_{z})\big)P_{j}^{0}(\cos\!\theta)\Bigg)\ .

(121)

Projecting out the Legendre polynomials one by one, the coefficients

A_{\ell,j}^{0,0}(q\mathbf{e}_{z})
obey

0=\alpha_{\ell,j-1}q\frac{j}{\sqrt{2j-1}}+\alpha_{\ell,j+1}q\frac{j+1}{\sqrt{2j+3}}+\alpha_{\ell,j}\sqrt{2j+1}\big(j(j+1)-\lambda_{\ell}^{0}\big)

(122)

where we have used

\alpha_{\ell,j}=\mathcal{N}_{\ell}^{0}A_{\ell,j}^{0,0}(q\mathbf{e}_{z})\ ,

(123)

with some normalisation
\mathcal{N}_{\ell}^{0}
to be used below,
and
\lambda_{\ell}=\lambda_{\ell}^{0}(q\mathbf{e}_{z})

to ease notation.

Eq. (
122
) can of course be written in matrix form

\begin{pmatrix}0&\frac{q}{\sqrt{3}}&0&\\
\frac{q}{\sqrt{3}}&2&\frac{2q}{\sqrt{15}}&\ldots\\
0&\frac{2q}{\sqrt{15}}&6&\\
&\vdots&&\ddots\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\alpha_{\ell,0}\\
\alpha_{\ell,1}\\
\alpha_{\ell,2}\\
\vdots\end{pmatrix}=\lambda_{\ell}^{0}(q\mathbf{e}_{z})\begin{pmatrix}\alpha_{\ell,0}\\
\alpha_{\ell,1}\\
\alpha_{\ell,2}\\
\vdots\end{pmatrix}

(124)

By demanding that this equation is to be solved to a certain order in
q
and by
assuming

\alpha_{\ell,j}\in\mathcal{O}\!\left(q^{|\ell-j|}\right)
, only a finite number of matrix elements and vector elements need to be considered at a time, while also constraining the order of the eigenvalue. Using Eq. (
116
) as the starting point, the first order equation for
\ell=0
is

\begin{pmatrix}0&\frac{q}{\sqrt{3}}\\
\frac{q}{\sqrt{3}}&2\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}1\\
u_{1}q\end{pmatrix}=(0+vq)\begin{pmatrix}1\\
u_{1}q\end{pmatrix}+\mathcal{O}\!\left(q^{2}\right)\ ,

(125)

where we have labelled the unknown term of
\alpha_{0,1}
by
u_{1}q
and the unknown term of
\lambda_{0}^{0}(q\mathbf{e}_{z})
by
vq
.
The
0
in the eigenvalue on the right hand side of Eq. (
125
) is that of
\lambda_{\ell}^{0}
at
\ell=0
, Eq. (
116b
). Focussing solely on terms of order
q
produces two equations,
0=vq
and
q/\sqrt{3}+2u_{1}q=0
, determining both
v=0
and
u_{1}=-1/(2\sqrt{3})
. With those in place, the next order equation is

\begin{pmatrix}0&\frac{q}{\sqrt{3}}&0\\
\frac{q}{\sqrt{3}}&2&\frac{2q}{\sqrt{15}}\\
0&\frac{2q}{\sqrt{15}}&6\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}1\\
-\frac{q}{2\sqrt{3}}+u_{1}q^{2}\\
u_{2}q^{2}\end{pmatrix}=(0+vq^{2})\begin{pmatrix}1\\
-\frac{q}{2\sqrt{3}}+u_{1}q^{2}\\
u_{2}q^{2}\end{pmatrix}+\mathcal{O}\!\left(q^{3}\right)\ ,

(126)

determining
u_{1}
,
u_{2}
and
v
, now labelling terms of order
q^{2}
.

The only additional difficulty for
\ell>0
is that the number of rows and columns of the matrix to be considered going from one order to the next may increase by
2
for the first
\ell
orders. For example the first non-trivial order for
\ell=1
is

\begin{pmatrix}0&\frac{q}{\sqrt{3}}&0\\
\frac{q}{\sqrt{3}}&2&\frac{2q}{\sqrt{15}}\\
0&\frac{2q}{\sqrt{15}}&6\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}u_{0}q\\
1\\
u_{2}q\end{pmatrix}=(2+vq)\begin{pmatrix}u_{0}q\\
1\\
u_{2}q\end{pmatrix}+\mathcal{O}\!\left(q^{2}\right)\ .

(127)

With this process, we have determined

\Bigg[\begin{pmatrix}0&\frac{q}{\sqrt{3}}&0&0&0\\
\frac{q}{\sqrt{3}}&2&\frac{2q}{\sqrt{15}}&0&0\\
0&\frac{2q}{\sqrt{15}}&6&\frac{3q}{\sqrt{35}}&0\\
0&0&\frac{3q}{\sqrt{35}}&12&\frac{4q}{\sqrt{63}}\\
0&0&0&\frac{4q}{\sqrt{63}}&20\\
\end{pmatrix}-\left(-\frac{q^{2}}{6}+\frac{11q^{4}}{1080}\right)\mathds{1}\Bigg]\begin{pmatrix}1\\
-\frac{q}{2\sqrt{3}}+\frac{11q^{3}}{360\sqrt{3}}\\
\frac{q^{2}}{18\sqrt{5}}-\frac{43q^{4}}{9072\sqrt{5}}\\
-\frac{q^{3}}{360\sqrt{7}}\\
\frac{q^{4}}{37800}\end{pmatrix}\in\mathcal{O}\!\left(q^{5}\right)\qquad\qquad\text{for }\ell=0\ ,

(128)

which can be improved further by demanding that the term of order
q^{6}
in
\lambda_{0}^{0}
is consistent with
\alpha_{0,2}=q^{2}/(18\sqrt{5})-43q^{4}/(9072\sqrt{5})
in Eq. (
128
), producing

\lambda_{0}^{0}(q\mathbf{e}_{z})=-\frac{q^{2}}{6}+\frac{11q^{4}}{1080}-\frac{47q^{6}}{34020}+\mathcal{O}\!\left(q^{7}\right)\qquad\text{for }\ell=0\ .

(129)

For
\ell=1
, we found

\Bigg[\begin{pmatrix}0&\frac{q}{\sqrt{3}}&0&0&0\\
\frac{q}{\sqrt{3}}&2&\frac{2q}{\sqrt{15}}&0&0\\
0&\frac{2q}{\sqrt{15}}&6&\frac{3q}{\sqrt{35}}&0\\
0&0&\frac{3q}{\sqrt{35}}&12&\frac{4q}{\sqrt{63}}\\
0&0&0&\frac{4q}{\sqrt{63}}&20\\
\end{pmatrix}-\left(2+\frac{q^{2}}{10}\right)\mathds{1}\Bigg]\begin{pmatrix}\frac{q}{2\sqrt{3}}-\frac{q^{3}}{40\sqrt{3}}\\
1\\
-\frac{q}{2\sqrt{15}}-\frac{11q^{3}}{700\sqrt{15}}\\
\frac{3q^{2}}{100\sqrt{21}}\\
-\frac{q^{3}}{3150\sqrt{3}}\end{pmatrix}\in\mathcal{O}\!\left(q^{4}\right)\qquad\qquad\text{for }\ell=1\ ,

(130)

i.e.

\lambda_{1}^{0}(q\mathbf{e}_{z})=2+\frac{q^{2}}{10}+\mathcal{O}\!\left(q^{4}\right)\qquad\text{for }\ell=1\ .

(131)

Matrix expressions such as Eqs. (
128
) and (
130
) are easily verified with a computer algebra system, simply by determining whether the remainder has the expected order.

Eqs. (
113
) and (
115
) imply

\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\sum_{k=-j}^{j}\left(A_{\ell,j}^{m,k}(q\mathbf{e}_{z})\right)^{2}=1

(132)

which determines the normalization
\mathcal{N}_{\ell}^{0}
in Eq. (
123
) and with Eq. (
128
) this produces the desired coefficients for
\ell=0
,

\displaystyle A_{0,0}^{0,0}(q\mathbf{e}_{z})

\displaystyle=1-\frac{q^{2}}{24}+\frac{383q^{4}}{51840}+\mathcal{O}\!\left(q^{5}\right)

(133a)

\displaystyle A_{0,1}^{0,0}(q\mathbf{e}_{z})

\displaystyle=-\frac{q}{2\sqrt{3}}+\frac{37q^{3}}{720\sqrt{3}}+\mathcal{O}\!\left(q^{5}\right)

(133b)

\displaystyle A_{0,2}^{0,0}(q\mathbf{e}_{z})

\displaystyle=\frac{q^{2}}{18\sqrt{5}}-\frac{4q^{4}}{567\sqrt{5}}+\mathcal{O}\!\left(q^{5}\right)

(133c)

\displaystyle A_{0,3}^{0,0}(q\mathbf{e}_{z})

\displaystyle=-\frac{q^{3}}{360\sqrt{7}}+\mathcal{O}\!\left(q^{5}\right)

(133d)

\displaystyle A_{0,4}^{0,0}(q\mathbf{e}_{z})

\displaystyle=\frac{q^{4}}{37800}+\mathcal{O}\!\left(q^{5}\right)\ .

(133e)

Similarly, we found for
\ell=1
from Eq. (
130
),

\displaystyle A_{1,0}^{0,0}(q\mathbf{e}_{z})

\displaystyle=\frac{q}{2\sqrt{3}}-\frac{q^{3}}{20\sqrt{3}}+\mathcal{O}\!\left(q^{4}\right)

(134a)

\displaystyle A_{1,1}^{0,0}(q\mathbf{e}_{z})

\displaystyle=1-\frac{q^{2}}{20}+\mathcal{O}\!\left(q^{4}\right)

(134b)

\displaystyle A_{1,2}^{0,0}(q\mathbf{e}_{z})

\displaystyle=-\frac{q}{2\sqrt{15}}+\frac{13q^{3}}{1400\sqrt{15}}+\mathcal{O}\!\left(q^{4}\right)

(134c)

\displaystyle A_{1,3}^{0,0}(q\mathbf{e}_{z})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{100}\sqrt{\frac{3}{7}}q^{2}+\mathcal{O}\!\left(q^{4}\right)\ .

(134d)

For general
\ell
we observe that in deed

A_{\ell,0}^{0,0}(q\mathbf{e}_{z})\in\mathcal{O}\!\left(q^{\ell}\right)

(135)

by construction.

Finally, we calculate the integral of
u_{\ell}^{m}\left(\theta,\varphi,\mathbf{q}(q\mathbf{e}_{z})\right)
over
\varphi
and
\theta
, Eq. (
77
). Firstly, taking the integral over
\varphi
of the definition Eq. (
119
) enforces
m=0
,

\int_{0}^{\pi}\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}\theta\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\theta\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\theta\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\theta\,}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}\varphi\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\varphi\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\varphi\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\varphi\,}\sin\!\theta u_{\ell}^{m}\left(\theta,\varphi,\mathbf{q}(\mathbf{k})\right)=2\pi\delta_{m,0}\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}A_{\ell,j}^{0,0}(\mathbf{q}(\mathbf{k}))\sqrt{2j+1}\int_{0}^{\pi}\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}\theta\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\theta\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\theta\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\theta\,}\sin\!\theta\,P_{j}^{0}(\cos\!\theta)\ .

(136)

Using the orthogonality of the Legendre polynomials,

\int_{0}^{\pi}\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}\theta\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\theta\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\theta\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\theta\,}\sin\!\theta\,P_{j}^{m}(\cos\!\theta)P_{k}^{m}(\cos\!\theta)=\frac{2(j+m)!}{(2j+1)(j-m)!}\delta_{j,k}

(137)

and
P_{0}^{0}=1
, which means that the integral of
P_{j}^{0}
is its projection against
P_{0}^{0}=1
, then gives the desired

\int_{0}^{\pi}\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}\theta\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\theta\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\theta\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\theta\,}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}\varphi\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\varphi\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\varphi\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\varphi\,}\sin\!\theta u_{\ell}^{m}\left(\theta,\varphi,\mathbf{q}(\mathbf{k})\right)=4\pi\delta_{m,0}A_{\ell,0}^{0,0}(\mathbf{q}(\mathbf{k}))\ .

(138)

Appendix B
Clebsch-Gordan coefficients

A product of two spherical harmonics can be expanded as

Y_{\ell}^{m}(\theta,\varphi)Y_{\ell^{\prime}}^{m^{\prime}}(\theta,\varphi)=\sum_{L,M}\sqrt{\frac{(2\ell+1)(2\ell^{\prime}+1)}{4\pi(2L+1)}}c_{\ell^{\prime},0,\ell,0}^{L,0}c_{\ell^{\prime},m^{\prime},\ell,m}^{L,M}Y_{L}^{M}(\theta,\varphi)\ ,

(139)

where
c_{\ell^{\prime},m^{\prime},\ell,m}^{L,M}
are the Clebsch-Gordan coefficients
[
15
]
.
By rewriting trigonometric functions in Eq. (
98
) as spherical harmonics,

\displaystyle\sin\!\theta\cos\varphi

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\frac{2\pi}{3}}\bigg(-Y_{1}^{1}({\theta,\varphi})+Y_{1}^{-1}({\theta,\varphi})\bigg)

(140a)

\displaystyle\sin\!\theta\sin\!\theta

\displaystyle=\mathring{\imath}\sqrt{\frac{2\pi}{3}}\bigg(Y_{1}^{1}({\theta,\varphi})+Y_{1}^{-1}({\theta,\varphi})\bigg)

(140b)

\displaystyle\cos\!\theta

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\frac{4\pi}{3}}Y_{1}^{0}(\theta,\varphi)\ ,

(140c)

and using Eq. (
139
), we arrive at Eq. (
99
).

We list the Clebsch-Gordan coefficients of Eq. (
99
),

\displaystyle c_{1,0,\ell,0}^{L,0}

\displaystyle=\delta_{L,\ell+1}\sqrt{\frac{1+\ell}{1+2\ell}}-\delta_{L,\ell-1}\sqrt{\frac{\ell}{1+2\ell}}\ ,

(141a)

\displaystyle c_{1,0,\ell,m}^{L,M}

\displaystyle=\delta_{m,M}\bigg(\delta_{L,\ell+1}\sqrt{\frac{(\ell+1-m)(\ell+1+m)}{(1+\ell)(1+2\ell)}}-\delta_{L,\ell}m\sqrt{\frac{1}{\ell(1+\ell)}}-\delta_{L,\ell-1}\sqrt{\frac{(\ell-m)(\ell+m)}{\ell(2\ell+1)}}\bigg)\ ,

(141b)

\displaystyle c_{1,1,\ell,m}^{L,M}

\displaystyle=\frac{\delta_{m+1,M}}{\sqrt{2}}\bigg(\delta_{L,\ell+1}\sqrt{\frac{(1+\ell+m)(2+\ell+m)}{(1+\ell)(1+2\ell)}}+\delta_{L,\ell-1}\sqrt{\frac{(\ell-1-m)(\ell-m)}{\ell(1+2\ell)}}+\delta_{L,\ell}\sqrt{\frac{(\ell-m)(1+\ell+m)}{\ell(1+\ell)}}\bigg)\ ,

(141c)

\displaystyle c_{1,-1,\ell,m}^{L,M}

\displaystyle=\frac{\delta_{m-1,M}}{\sqrt{2}}\bigg(\delta_{L,\ell+1}\sqrt{\frac{(1+\ell-m)(2+\ell-m)}{(1+\ell)(1+2\ell)}}+\delta_{L,\ell-1}\sqrt{\frac{(\ell+m-1)(\ell+m)}{\ell(1+2\ell)}}-\delta_{L,\ell}\sqrt{\frac{(1+\ell-m)(\ell+m)}{\ell(1+\ell)}}\bigg)\ .

(141d)

By substituting the above Clebsch-Gordan coefficients into Eq. (
99
), the first two tumble vertices
W_{\ell^{\prime},\ell}^{m^{\prime},m}
on the right hand side of Eq. (
100
) are

\displaystyle W_{\ell^{\prime},\ell}^{m^{\prime},m}(k_{x}\mathbf{e}_{x})

\displaystyle=-\mathring{\imath}w_{0}k_{x}\sum_{L,M}\sqrt{\frac{2\ell+1}{2(2L+1)}}c_{1,0,\ell,0}^{L,0}\bigg(-c_{1,1,\ell,m}^{L,M}+c_{1,-1,\ell,m}^{L,M}\bigg)\delta_{m^{\prime},M}\delta_{\ell^{\prime},L}

(142a)

\displaystyle=-\mathring{\imath}w_{0}k_{x}\bigg\{\frac{\delta_{m^{\prime},m+1}}{2}\bigg(-\delta_{\ell^{\prime},\ell+1}\sqrt{\frac{(1+\ell+m)(2+\ell+m)}{(2\ell+3)(2\ell+1)}}+\delta_{\ell^{\prime},\ell-1}\sqrt{\frac{(\ell-1-m)(\ell-m)}{(2\ell-1)(2\ell+1)}}\bigg)

(142b)

\displaystyle\quad\quad+\frac{\delta_{m^{\prime},m-1}}{2}\bigg(\delta_{\ell^{\prime},\ell+1}\sqrt{\frac{(1+\ell-m)(2+\ell-m)}{(2\ell+3)(2\ell+1)}}-\delta_{\ell^{\prime},\ell-1}\sqrt{\frac{(\ell+m)(\ell+m-1)}{(2\ell+1)(2\ell-1)}}\bigg)\bigg\}\ ,

(142c)

\displaystyle W_{\ell^{\prime},\ell}^{m^{\prime},m}(k_{y}\mathbf{e}_{y})

\displaystyle=w_{0}k_{y}\sum_{L,M}\sqrt{\frac{2\ell+1}{2(2L+1)}}c_{1,0,\ell,0}^{L,0}\bigg(c_{1,1,\ell,m}^{L,M}+c_{1,-1,\ell,m}^{L,M}\bigg)\delta_{m^{\prime},M}\delta_{\ell^{\prime},L}

(142d)

\displaystyle=w_{0}k_{y}\bigg\{\frac{\delta_{m^{\prime},m+1}}{2}\bigg(\delta_{\ell^{\prime},\ell+1}\sqrt{\frac{(1+\ell+m)(2+\ell+m)}{(2\ell+3)(2\ell+1)}}-\delta_{\ell^{\prime},\ell-1}\sqrt{\frac{(\ell-1-m)(\ell-m)}{(2\ell-1)(2\ell+1)}}\bigg)

(142e)

\displaystyle\quad\quad+\frac{\delta_{m^{\prime},m-1}}{2}\bigg(\delta_{\ell^{\prime},\ell+1}\sqrt{\frac{(1+\ell-m)(2+\ell-m)}{(2\ell+3)(2\ell+1)}}-\delta_{\ell^{\prime},\ell-1}\sqrt{\frac{(\ell+m)(\ell+m-1)}{(2\ell+1)(2\ell-1)}}\bigg)\bigg\}

(142f)

which together with the third term,
W_{\ell^{\prime},\ell}^{m^{\prime},m}(k_{z}\mathbf{e}_{z})
in Eq. (
101
) complete the right hand side of Eq. (
100
).

Appendix C
Field Theory of the Drift Diffusion in an Arbitrary Potential

In the following we derive the field theory for a drift-diffusion
particle in a finite volume with potential
\Upsilon(\mathbf{r})
and
determine the stationary particle density
\rho_{0}(\mathbf{r})
.
Our motivation is two-fold: Firstly, we want to reproduce the barometric formula
\rho_{0}(\mathbf{r})\propto\mathchoice{\mathrm{e}^{-\Upsilon(\mathbf{r})/\mathsf{D}_{t}}}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-\Upsilon(\mathbf{r})/\mathsf{D}_{t}\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-\Upsilon(\mathbf{r})/\mathsf{D}_{t}\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-\Upsilon(\mathbf{r})/\mathsf{D}_{t}\right)}
in the absence of drift, which is the equilibrium situation of pure diffusion, where the diffusion constant
\mathsf{D}_{t}
plays the role of the temperature. The barometric formula is Boltzmann’s factor, which we want to see reproduced by the field theory as a matter of consistency, Section
C.4
. The derivation will provide us with a template for the more complicated case of a drift.
Secondly, we want to determine precisely those corrections due to a constant drift with velocity
\mathbf{w}_{0}
. We will compare our field-theoretic, perturbative result with the classical calculation in one dimension in Section
C.5
.

We study this phenomenon in a
finite
volume with linear extent
L
, so that the system develops into a stationary state even in the absence of an external potential, which will be treated perturbatively. In a finite volume, the Fourier series representation of any reasonable (finite, continuous, more generally fulfilling the Dirichlet conditions) potential exists. In an infinite volume, we would have to demand that the potential vanishes at large distances, which may result in the stationary density no longer being normalisable even in the presence of the potential.

To simplify the calculations, we further demand the domain to be periodic.
The annihilation field
\chi(\mathbf{r},t)
and the
Doi-shifted creation field
\tilde{\chi}(\mathbf{r},t)
at position
\mathbf{r}
and time
t
can then be written as

\displaystyle\tilde{\chi}(\mathbf{r},t)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{L^{d}}\sum_{\mathbf{n}\in\mathds{Z}^{d}}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega\,}\mathchoice{\mathrm{e}^{-\mathring{\imath}\omega t}}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-\mathring{\imath}\omega t\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-\mathring{\imath}\omega t\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-\mathring{\imath}\omega t\right)}\mathchoice{\mathrm{e}^{\mathring{\imath}\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}}\cdot\mathbf{r}}}{\operatorname{exp}\left(\mathring{\imath}\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}}\cdot\mathbf{r}\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(\mathring{\imath}\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}}\cdot\mathbf{r}\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(\mathring{\imath}\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}}\cdot\mathbf{r}\right)}\tilde{\chi}(\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}},\omega)

(143a)

\displaystyle\chi(\mathbf{r},t)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{L^{d}}\sum_{\mathbf{n}\in\mathds{Z}^{d}}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega\,}\mathchoice{\mathrm{e}^{-\mathring{\imath}\omega t}}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-\mathring{\imath}\omega t\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-\mathring{\imath}\omega t\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-\mathring{\imath}\omega t\right)}\mathchoice{\mathrm{e}^{\mathring{\imath}\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}}\cdot\mathbf{r}}}{\operatorname{exp}\left(\mathring{\imath}\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}}\cdot\mathbf{r}\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(\mathring{\imath}\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}}\cdot\mathbf{r}\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(\mathring{\imath}\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}}\cdot\mathbf{r}\right)}\chi(\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}},\omega)

(143b)

with the notation in keeping with the rest of the present work. The finite volume we have chosen to be a cube with linear extension
L
, easily generalisable to a cuboid or similar. The Fourier modes are
\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}}=2\pi\mathbf{n}/L
with
\mathbf{n}=(n_{1},n_{2},\ldots,n_{d})^{\mathsf{T}}
a
d
-dimensional integer in
\mathds{Z}^{d}
. We further use the notation
\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22=\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}/\,}{\!\mathrm{d}/\,}{\!\mathrm{d}/\,}{\!\mathrm{d}/\,}\!(2\pi)
and later, correspondingly,
\delta\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22(\omega)=2\pi\delta(\omega)
to cancel that factor of
2\pi
.

The time-independent potential
\Upsilon(\mathbf{r})
follows the convention Eq. (
30
), restated here for convenience in arbitrary dimensions

\displaystyle\Upsilon(\mathbf{r})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{L^{d}}\sum_{\mathbf{n}\in\mathds{Z}^{d}}\Upsilon_{\mathbf{n}}\mathchoice{\mathrm{e}^{\mathring{\imath}\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}}\cdot\mathbf{r}}}{\operatorname{exp}\left(\mathring{\imath}\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}}\cdot\mathbf{r}\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(\mathring{\imath}\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}}\cdot\mathbf{r}\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(\mathring{\imath}\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}}\cdot\mathbf{r}\right)}

(144a)

\displaystyle\Upsilon_{\mathbf{n}}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{L}\ldots\int_{0}^{L}\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}^{d}r\,}{\!\mathrm{d}^{d}r\,}{\!\mathrm{d}^{d}r\,}{\!\mathrm{d}^{d}r\,}\Upsilon(\mathbf{r})\mathchoice{\mathrm{e}^{-\mathring{\imath}\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}}\cdot\mathbf{r}}}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-\mathring{\imath}\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}}\cdot\mathbf{r}\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-\mathring{\imath}\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}}\cdot\mathbf{r}\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-\mathring{\imath}\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}}\cdot\mathbf{r}\right)}\ .

(144b)

C.1
Action

The Fokker-Planck equation of the fully time-dependent density
\rho(\mathbf{r},t)
of particles at position
\mathbf{r}
and time
t
, subject to diffusion with constant
\mathsf{D}_{t}
and drift with velocity
\mathbf{w}
, in a potential
\Upsilon(\mathbf{r})
can be written as

\partial_{t}\rho(\mathbf{r},t)=\mathsf{D}_{t}\nabla_{\mathbf{r}}^{2}\rho(\mathbf{r},t)-\nabla_{\mathbf{r}}\cdot\big(\rho(\mathbf{r},t)(\mathbf{w}-\nabla\Upsilon(\mathbf{r}))\big)

(145)

removing all ambiguity about the action of the gradient operators by stating that they act on all objects to their right.
The action of particles with diffusion constant
\mathsf{D}_{t}
and moving ballistically with velocity
\mathbf{w}_{0}
is
[
8
]

\mathcal{A}_{0}[\tilde{\chi},\chi]=-\int\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega\,}\frac{1}{L^{d}}\sum_{\mathbf{n}\in\mathds{Z}^{d}}\tilde{\chi}(-\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}},-\omega)\chi(\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}},\omega)\big(-\mathring{\imath}\omega+\mathsf{D}_{t}k_{\mathbf{n}}^{2}+\mathring{\imath}\mathbf{w}_{0}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}}+r\big)

(146)

with
k_{\mathbf{n}}=|\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}}|
and
r\downarrow 0
a mass to regularise the infrared and establish causality. An additional external potential results in an additional space-dependent drift term and thus in the action

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{P}

\displaystyle=\int\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}^{d}r\,}{\!\mathrm{d}^{d}r\,}{\!\mathrm{d}^{d}r\,}{\!\mathrm{d}^{d}r\,}\int\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}t\,}{\!\mathrm{d}t\,}{\!\mathrm{d}t\,}{\!\mathrm{d}t\,}\tilde{\chi}(\mathbf{r},t)\nabla_{\mathbf{r}}\cdot\big(\nabla_{\mathbf{r}}\Upsilon(\mathbf{r})\big)\chi(\mathbf{r},t)

(147a)

\displaystyle=\int\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega\,}\frac{1}{L^{3d}}\sum_{\mathbf{n}_{1},\mathbf{n}_{2},\mathbf{n}_{3}}\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}_{1}}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}_{2}}\,\tilde{\chi}(\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}_{1}},-\omega)\Upsilon_{\mathbf{n}_{2}}\chi(\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}_{3}},\omega)L^{d}\delta_{\mathbf{n}_{1}+\mathbf{n}_{2}+\mathbf{n}_{3},\mathbf{0}}

(147b)

e.g.
Eqs. (
9b
) or (
29a
)
to be treated perturbatively, Eq. (
8
).

The bare propagator follows immediately from Eq. (
146
),

\left\langle\chi(\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}},\omega)\tilde{\chi}(\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{p}},\omega_{0})\right\rangle_{0}=\frac{\delta\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22(\omega+\omega_{0})L^{d}\delta_{\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{p},\mathbf{0}}}{-\mathring{\imath}\omega+\mathsf{D}_{t}k_{\mathbf{n}}^{2}+\mathring{\imath}\mathbf{w}_{0}\cdot\mathbf{k}+r}=\delta\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22(\omega+\omega_{0})L^{d}\delta_{\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{p},\mathbf{0}}G\left(\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}},\omega\right)\triangleq\hbox to59.27pt{\vbox to17.33pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 28.93373pt\lower-0.59999pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {

(148)

and the perturbative part of the action provides a single vertex with a “bauble”

to be summed over.

(150)

\displaystyle=\delta\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22(\omega+\omega_{0})\Big\{L^{d}\delta_{\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{p},0}G\left(\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}},\omega\right)+G\left(\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}},\omega\right)\mathbf{k}_{-\mathbf{n}}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{p}}\Upsilon_{\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{p}}G\left(-\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{p}},\omega\right)+\ldots\Big\}\ .

(151)

More systematically, the full propagator may be expanded as

\left\langle\chi(\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}},\omega)\tilde{\chi}(-\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{p}},\omega_{0})\right\rangle=\delta\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22(\omega+\omega_{0})\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}E_{j}(\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}},\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{p}},\omega)

(152)

where we changed the sign of
\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{p}}
in the creator field compared to Eq. (
150
) to improve notation below.
The expansion terms
E_{j}
are defined as follows

with the general recurrence

E_{j+1}(\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}},\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{p}},\omega)=-G\left(\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}},\omega\right)\frac{1}{L^{d}}\sum_{\mathbf{q}\in\mathds{Z}^{d}}\big(\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}-\mathbf{q}}\big)\Upsilon_{\mathbf{n}-\mathbf{q}}E_{j}(\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{q}},\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{p}},\omega)

(154)

amounting to a convolution to be analysed further below.

C.3
Stationary state

The density
\rho_{0}(\mathbf{r})
in the stationary or steady state is found by taking of the propagator the limit
t_{0}\to-\infty
of the time of the initial deposition,

\rho_{0}(\mathbf{r})=\lim_{t_{0}\to-\infty}\left\langle\chi(\mathbf{r},t)\tilde{\chi}(\mathbf{r}_{0},t_{0})\right\rangle

(155)

expected to be independent of
\mathbf{r}_{0}
and
t
, provided the system is ergodic and possesses a (unique) stationary state.
Provided all internal legs are dashed,
i.e.
they carry a factor
\mathbf{k}
,
the diagrammatic mechanics of this limit amounts to an amputation of the incoming leg in any composite diagram. This is most easily understood by taking the inverse Fourier transform from
\omega,\omega_{0}
to
t,t_{0}
, while leaving the spatial dependence in
\mathbf{k}
, starting from the propagators that have the form Eq. (
148
). The inverse Fourier transform will result in exponentials of the form
\mathchoice{\mathrm{e}^{-\mathring{\imath}t_{0}s}}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-\mathring{\imath}t_{0}s\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-\mathring{\imath}t_{0}s\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-\mathring{\imath}t_{0}s\right)}
, where
s
is the location of the pole of the propagators, here
s=\mathring{\imath}(\mathsf{D}_{t}k_{\mathbf{n}}^{2}+\mathring{\imath}\mathbf{w}_{0}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}}+r)
with
\Im{(s)}\geq 0
. Only if the imaginary part of
s
vanishes, the exponential will not vanish as
t_{0}\to-\infty
. This happens only when
\mathbf{n}=\mathbf{0}
and in the limit of
r\downarrow 0
. The resulting residue is thus determined by the remainder of the diagram evaluated at the pole
\omega=0
and with the incoming leg replaced by
\delta_{\mathbf{p},\mathbf{0}}
, so that an inverse Fourier transform in
\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{p}}
to
\mathbf{r}_{0}
results in a factor
1/L^{d}
. As for the individual propagator Eq. (
153a
) in Eq. (
152
), it contributes indeed only this background,
1/L^{d}
. Writing therefore
E_{i}
with only one arguments as the desired limit,

\displaystyle E_{i}(\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}})

allows the stationary density to be written in the form

\rho_{0}(\mathbf{r})=\frac{1}{L^{d}}\sum_{\mathbf{n}\in\mathds{Z}}\mathchoice{\mathrm{e}^{\mathring{\imath}\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}}\cdot\mathbf{r}}}{\operatorname{exp}\left(\mathring{\imath}\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}}\cdot\mathbf{r}\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(\mathring{\imath}\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}}\cdot\mathbf{r}\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(\mathring{\imath}\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}}\cdot\mathbf{r}\right)}\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}E_{j}(\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}})

(157)

via Eqs. (
152
) and (
155
)
and gives

E_{0}(\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}})=L^{d}\delta_{\mathbf{n},\mathbf{0}}\frac{1}{L^{d}}=\delta_{\mathbf{n},\mathbf{0}}

(158)

from Eq. (
153a
) and

E_{1}(\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}})=-G\left(\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}},0\right)\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}}\mathcal{I}(\mathbf{n}\neq\mathbf{0})\Upsilon_{\mathbf{n}}\frac{1}{L^{d}}=-\frac{1}{L^{d}}\frac{k_{\mathbf{n}}^{2}\Upsilon_{\mathbf{n}}\mathcal{I}(\mathbf{n}\neq\mathbf{0})}{\mathsf{D}_{t}k_{\mathbf{n}}^{2}+\mathring{\imath}\mathbf{w}_{0}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}}}\ ,

(159)

using Eq. (
148
) and
where

\mathcal{I}(\mathbf{n}\neq\mathbf{0})=1-\delta_{\mathbf{n},\mathbf{0}}

(160)

accounts for the subtlety that the right hand side of Eq. (
153b
) strictly vanishes for
\mathbf{n}=\mathbf{0}
, because of the
\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}}
prefactor and all other terms being finite as
r\neq 0
. However, in Eq. (
156
) the limit
r\downarrow 0
has to be taken so that
t_{0}\to-\infty
produces a finite result, resulting in

\lim_{r\downarrow 0}\frac{\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}}}{\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}}^{2}+r}=\frac{\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}}}{\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}}^{2}}\mathcal{I}(\mathbf{n}\neq\mathbf{0})=\begin{cases}\mathbf{0}&\text{for $\mathbf{k}=\mathbf{0}$}\\
\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}}/\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}}^{2}&\text{otherwise}\ ,\end{cases}

(161)

using the convention
0\infty=0
. The indicator function Eq. (
160
) needs to be in place for every prefactor
\mathbf{k}
that also features in the denominator of a propagator
G\left(\mathbf{k},0\right)
so that the limits
r\downarrow 0
and
t\rightarrow\infty
can safely be taken.

For easier comparison, we state the expansion of the density to first order in the potential for drift-diffusion particles in a finite volume determined explicitly so far,

\rho_{0}(\mathbf{r})=\frac{1}{L^{d}}-\frac{1}{L^{2d}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{n}\in\mathds{Z}\\
\mathbf{n}\neq\mathbf{0}\end{subarray}}\mathchoice{\mathrm{e}^{\mathring{\imath}\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}}\cdot\mathbf{r}}}{\operatorname{exp}\left(\mathring{\imath}\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}}\cdot\mathbf{r}\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(\mathring{\imath}\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}}\cdot\mathbf{r}\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(\mathring{\imath}\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}}\cdot\mathbf{r}\right)}\frac{k_{\mathbf{n}}^{2}\Upsilon_{\mathbf{n}}}{\mathsf{D}_{t}k_{\mathbf{n}}^{2}+\mathring{\imath}\mathbf{w}_{0}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}}}+\ldots

(162)

using Eqs. (
157
), (
158
) and (
159
).

With the indicator function Eq. (
160
) suppressing unwanted singularities,
the recurrence Eq. (
154
) remains essentially unchanged at staionarity, as only the outgoing propagator needs to be adjusted to
\omega=0
,

\displaystyle E_{j+1}(\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}})

\displaystyle=-G\left(\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}},0\right)\frac{1}{L^{d}}\sum_{\mathbf{q}\in\mathds{Z}^{d}}\mathcal{I}(\mathbf{n}\neq\mathbf{0})\big(\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}-\mathbf{q}}\big)\Upsilon_{\mathbf{n}-\mathbf{q}}E_{j}(\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{q}})

(163a)

\displaystyle=-\frac{\mathcal{I}(\mathbf{n}\neq\mathbf{0})}{\mathsf{D}_{t}k_{\mathbf{n}}^{2}+\mathring{\imath}\mathbf{w}_{0}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}}}\frac{1}{L^{d}}\sum_{\mathbf{q}\in\mathds{Z}^{d}}\big(\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{n}-\mathbf{q}}\big)\Upsilon_{\mathbf{n}-\mathbf{q}}E_{j}(\mathbf{k}_{\mathbf{q}})

(163b)
